LIVE – Will CPI Cause a Sell-Off? Asian Stocks and Bitcoin Face Fresh Test as NVIDIA Anxiety Grows

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Will CPI cause a sell-off across Asian stocks and Bitcoin? Asian stocks staged a sharp recovery this morning, but another US inflation report could put the rebound to the test. Investors are preparing for October’s Consumer Price Index (CPI), with expectations of slower monthly inflation offering some relief. However, rising Federal Reserve rate-hike bets and NVIDIA shareholders suing Jensen Huang have kept markets cautious.

The Korea’s KOSPI jumped 4.5% this morning trading, led by Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix. Hong Kong’s Hang Seng Index also advanced, while Singapore’s Straits Times Index joined the regional recovery. Bitcoin, meanwhile, remains below $75,000 ahead of the US inflation release.

South Korean stocks getting sent 📈 🤑 🥳 pic.twitter.com/KYd5V9Oh9d

Does this recovery have enough momentum to survive another inflation surprise? A hotter reading could revive concerns about borrowing costs, while weaker inflation might give risk assets room to recover. Will the latest CPI figures determine if the rebound continues, or will it cause a sell-off?

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

The US Bureau of Labor Statistics is scheduled to publish October CPI at 8:30 a.m. ET on November 10. Economists expect headline inflation to increase 0.3% month-over-month, slowing from September’s 0.6% rise. Core CPI, which excludes food and energy, is forecast to increase 0.2%, compared with 0.3% previously.

Last year’s government shutdown prevented the BLS from collecting the necessary October 2025 data. Economists nevertheless estimate headline inflation would have reached 3.9% annually.

The stakes remain high because markets have increasingly priced in another Federal Reserve rate hike. Traders recently assigned an 84% to 85% probability to a December increase, following stronger employment data. The Fed’s benchmark interest rate currently stands at 3.75% to 4%.

A hotter-than-expected reading could strengthen the case for tighter monetary policy. Higher Treasury yields and a stronger dollar would potentially pressure Asian equities, particularly technology companies with valuations sensitive to financing costs.

South Korea’s KOSPI could face the greatest exposure. Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix are central to the global semiconductor supply chain, making the index particularly sensitive to changes in AI investment sentiment. Both companies helped drive today’s rebound, with their shares rising 5.1% and 6.8%, respectively.

Hong Kong’s Hang Seng Index offers another important benchmark. Its technology-heavy components could face renewed pressure if investors reduce exposure to growth stocks. Singapore’s Straits Times Index provides a useful contrast, given its greater exposure to financial institutions and dividend-paying companies.

A cooler reading could produce the opposite reaction, easing rate concerns and supporting another regional advance.

Bitcoin is trading around $74,600 ahead of the inflation release, leaving it close to a crucial support zone. Our analysts identified the $73,000 level as an important level, with a sustained break potentially exposing $70,000.

A hotter CPI reading could intensify selling pressure as investors reassess interest-rate expectations. Bitcoin’s recent weakness also reflects fading institutional demand, with US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds recording $1.28 billion in outflows last week.

A softer inflation report could trigger a relief rally, but it might not guarantee a sustained recovery. Stronger ETF demand and improved liquidity would still be necessary to support a meaningful reversal.

NVIDIA adds another complication. The chipmaker and CEO Jensen Huang is being sued in a proposed shareholder class action filed in California yesterday. Investor Mark Kistenmacher alleges that NVIDIA misled shareholders about Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based data center company Megaspeed.

BREAKING: Nvidia, $NVDA, and Jensen Huang are being sued in a class action lawsuit, accusing ‌them of defrauding shareholders by concealing Huang's ties with a suspected Chinese spy.The lawsuit claims that shareholders were misled about Huang's alleged relationship with a woman…

The complaint claims the alleged relationship created reputational and national security risks that investors should have known about. NVIDIA’s board had already announced an independent investigation, while the company had not immediately commented on the lawsuit.

NVIDIA shares fell 4.3% on Monday, erasing $190 billion in market cap. The decline arrived just as Asian semiconductor shares were attempting to recover from last week’s technology-led sell-off.

NVIDIA remains central to the AI investment cycle, and any deterioration in confidence surrounding the company could amplify concerns about semiconductor valuations and the sustainability of AI spending. That could leave Korean chipmakers particularly vulnerable if another inflation shock hits markets.

So, will CPI cause a sell-off? It depends on a few factors. We might need to watch core prices, Treasury yields, and the dollar for clues about the Federal Reserve’s next move. A cooler report could extend the recovery, but a hotter reading may expose how fragile confidence in Asian technology stocks and Bitcoin is.

"Après trois éliminations successives en Coupe de France aux tirs au but contre La Duchère (1-1, 3-4 t.a.b.), GOAL FC (1-1, 3-4 t.a.b.) et Bourg-en-Bresse (3-3, 3-4 tirs au but),   le GFA Rumilly Vallières a enfin brisé la spirale infernale. What Man United, with their seemingly addictive habit, must do now once Michael Carrick is sacked: IAN LADYMAN on Sir Jim Ratcliffe's trail of bad judgment and waste

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published: 02:00 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 07:53 EST, 10 November 2026

Those who were there will never forget the utter chaos of Michael Carrick’s last act at Old Trafford. But as the outgoing United manager clung on to his dignity like a life raft in those mad closing moments against Aston Villa, the current poster boy for United’s desperate demise sat on the bench in his tracksuit.

Diogo Dalot is an honest and by all accounts fully committed Portuguese full-back who has been a United player for more than eight years.

The 27-year-old was bought for his teenage potential by his compatriot Jose Mourinho in 2018 but has never trained on. He has never been good enough for a really top English club yet has made more than 250 appearances for the most famous one of all and has played for five different permanent managers.

On Sunday he was booed off by his own supporters when substituted after an hour. His had been a particularly grim performance but the boos were not about him, not really.

No they were not about Dalot but instead about the fundamental truth of what he represents. Namely a steady, grisly and unforgivable drain of footballing standards at United that actually began in the final days of Sir Alex Ferguson and has subsequently never stopped.

It is this that has taken United from Ferguson’s final valedictory title of 2013 to where they are today, the bottom three of the Premier League. It is this that has drained United of all their clout and reputation. It is this that has cost yet another United manager – a seventh – his job.

Diogo Dalot has become the poster boy for the decline under Michael Carrick as Man United seemingly plumb new depths

The outgoing club legend is not a good enough manager for United and his appointment was yet another mis-step by the club's hierarchy

Carrick is not a good enough manager for United. His appointment was an emotional mistake, one of many made by Sir Jim Ratcliffe and the band of misfits and hopefuls who are currently in charge of football operations.

With a better manager – a better coach – in charge of the current team, United would be in the top half of the league. The fact they sit 18th and resemble a Sunday League team that never gets going until they have shaken their hangovers off is squarely down to him.

Nevertheless, United are a football club that exists to win trophies, no matter what the majority owners the Glazers may have said recently. But United will not win the big trophies again until they buy better players and by that I mean better footballers and better people.

It sounds simple, trite even. But it’s a simple truth of our game. Until United can reverse a seemingly addictive habit of paying too much money for too little, the difference made by a manager will only ever be a matter of six or seven places in the Premier League standings. And United needs a much greater improvement than that until this vicious cycle of self-harm comes to an end.

There is so much wrong at the modern Manchester United away from the field. Much of it begins and ends with the hideous hubris and self-regard of Ratcliffe, a man so shot through with misplaced self-confidence that he still insists on being tailed everywhere by a journalist he has hired to write a book on United’s journey back to the top of the mountain. Suffice to say that one won’t be ready for this Christmas.

But the thread that links the final days of Ferguson and his chief executive David Gill through the years of Ed Woodward, Richard Arnold and John Murtough to these dark days of Ratcliffe’s INEOS control is simple. It’s sub-standard players.

Despite the financial drain of the Glazers’ stranglehold, United have spent in the region of £1.75bn on their first team in the last ten years.

It takes quite something to do that and take a club backwards. It takes quite something to do that and buy only one player – captain Bruno Fernandes – who can claim to have been consistently effective over his time at the club.

Somehow, though, United have managed it and it is this hideous trail of bad judgment and waste that follows the current team and those that have come before it every time it runs out on to the field.

United are weak off the pitch and even weaker on it. Their football mirrors their off-field strategy. Haphazard, wasteful and inconsistent.

A number of years ago – at a time when previous CEOs Woodward and Arnold were trying to mend a broken club - they enlisted the advice of Ferguson, Sir Bobby Charlton and former captain Bryan Robson to help them work out what exactly a United team should be.

‘Exciting, daring, confident and humble’ were the key words they laid down. Well, what on earth happened to any of that?

United have a huge decision to make – yet again – over a new manager. The field of suitable candidates is not as big as they would like. Nor is United as attractive a proposition as it was. The demands are enormous yet the foundations shake. It’s not a good combination.

A good coach would improve them. Carrick has had two central defenders on his staff in Jonny Evans and Jonathan Woodgate. What on earth have they been doing for the last two or three months?

Long before the arrival of Jim Ratcliffe former chief executive Ed Woodward and Richard Arnold attempted to draw a blueprint for the club

Their successors in the boardroom Omar Berrada and Jason Wilcox have not impressed over a baffling summer

But United remain four or five good transfer windows away from where they need to be and the last time they put a run of good business like that together at Old Trafford Carrick was probably in the first team.

United need proper talent but they also need proper people, not the kind who would rather moan to TV cameras after a defeat than go home and think about their own part in what has just happened.

Last summer’s strategy seemed to have come straight out of Chat GPT and reflects terribly on the current football director Jason Wilcox. To ‘fix’ the midfield through three signings with the need to ‘fix’ the back four kicked down the road for 12 months is not so much a strategy as an exercise in transfer suicide.

Some think Wilcox should follow Carrick through the door on the back of that and it’s a reasonable point of view. Viewed through the mist of an autumn afternoon on Sunday, Wilcox looked as grey as his recent track record. An alternative take is that the recruitment of players like Bryan Mbeumo and Matheus Cunha is indicative of an uptick in returns, at least in part.

Whichever path you wish to walk, it simply has to lead to an improvement in the one area that will either nourish or starve a football club. With that in mind, the first question a prospective manager should ask is who is buying the players and on the back of what long-term strategy.

Carrick’s United team have looked like exactly what they are this season, a group put together by different people following different principles for different managers. How strange that it hasn’t worked.

United, as we know, needed a left-back over the summer. Lewis Hall, of England and Newcastle, may have been an option. Instead, United placed their faith in Luke Shaw, a 31-year-old with a long history of injuries.

Shaw, unsurprisingly, is currently injured which brings us back to where we started, namely the sight of the right-footed Dalot labouring out of position on the left of Carrick’s back four against Villa on Sunday.

You could say we saw this coming. Many did. In the dressing room and in the stands at Old Trafford. Carrick? He knew about it, too, but then he was only the manager.

His exit will hurt for a while. He is an emotionally intelligent and empathetic individual who will reel from the trauma of the last ten weeks. Over time he will realise that he is well out if it, though. When he took the job last summer, Ferguson is known to have been concerned about what it may do to him. As often, Carrick’s old boss was right.

United are set up to fail and Carrick’s replacement will realise that soon enough. When he walks into the first team dressing room for the first time and looks for those upon whom he can rely to consistently help him, too many eyes will be focussed on the floor.

Derek McInnes will soon have a Rangers selection headache as two-striker approach assessed

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kris Boyd insists Ryan Naderi’s impressive form in front of goal has provided Derek McInnes with a selection headache he will be more than happy to have, with the striker stepping up in the absence of key attacking options.

The German forward has seized his opportunity in the starting line-up since club captain Lawrence Shankland and Youssef Chermiti were sidelined through injury, linking up effectively at the top end of the pitch with £9 million summer signing Kevin Kelsy.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

The pair have struck up a potent partnership in recent weeks, playing a key part in five successive wins that have provided renewed optimism surrounding Rangers’ title credentials this season.

Former Ibrox marksman Boyd has been impressed by Naderi’s all-round game and development after starting the season well down the pecking order, although he suggests there is still room for improvement.

“There's no doubt Chermiti was going to be the one at the top end of the pitch and maybe Shankland behind him,” Boyd said. “Kelsey has come in and he and Naderi have struck up a partnership.

“Shankland will be back soon. He is different to the other two and I still think he’s the best finisher at the club. But he will have a fight on his hands getting back in the team. It’s a good problem for Rangers to have.

“At the start of the season they were creating opportunities but weren’t taking them. You do feel they can score goals, but, more importantly, this team has more of a McInnes DNA defensively. Back then they had to score two, three or four goals to win a game. Right now they can get by with a 1-0. You’d want to kill it off a lot quicker than they do, but it was another three points [on Saturday] and defensively very good.”

Boyd is an advocate of McInnes' decision to play with two strikers, having operated in that system throughout his own career. He believes the approach provides Rangers with a greater physical presence and gives the opposition more to think about.

“Overall, both of them [Kelsy and Naderi] bring so much to the team and the way Derek wants to play. He wants to get the ball forward and it's two big boys who will ask questions of defenders. Both of them have been scoring. If you wanted to ask for more it would be goals from the wider areas.

“I played that way all my career but football has changed. Derek has always wanted to have two strikers though. Even when they were struggling at Kilmarnock he’d go two up top with wingers. I do think if you are one of the best sides in the country, you can have two strikers on the pitch as long as everyone is doing their work.”

The title race remains finely balanced heading into the final international break of the year, with Celtic four points ahead despite Rangers having a game in hand.

Boyd believes McInnes will welcome the opportunity to take stock of his team’s progress, even if they have continued to find ways to collect points.

“I don’t think he will be upset about the timing of the break,” he said. “Football is about momentum but you do need that break or reset to remind the players about the run they’ve been on. You always feel the last international break to the one in the New Year is when the league starts to sort itself out. Derek will want them refreshed to kick on towards the festive period.”

*Kris Boyd was speaking at the announcement of William Hill as Official Sponsor and Proper Supporter of Walking Football Scotland. To find your nearest game visit walkingfootballscotland.org.

Confession shows former favourite’s love affair with West Ham continues from afar

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It has taken three years for West Ham to spiral from the summit of their footballing prowess to the depths of second division mediocrity, and the Hammers’ experiences can serve as a cautionary tale to any other poorly run Premier League side. Complacency and poor decision making will destroy even the best – supported side in the blink of  an eye.

Hammers fans have also taken years to process the sale of their most successful captain since Billy Bonds, the Chairman announcing his availability the very day after their Europa Conference League trophy sale. For that alone, I’ll never forgive Sullivan.

There are ways of doing things. and that isn’t it.

As supporters we are now getting used to seeing Declan Rice in an Arsenal kit, and for many – most, I’d guess, but not all.. there’s a vicarious sense of pride still for the former Hammer, although of course not his club, as he goes onto deserved success.

It is clear the players’ love for West Ham hasn’t diminished either. Speaking to GQ magazine, Declan professed:

““I obviously love West Ham so much still, still a massive part of my life, I still watch them.”

Picking out his favourite goal ever, Rice glossed over those Champions League free kicks and confessed a goal in Claret and Blue meant more to him:

“My favourite goal is when I was playing for West Ham in the Conference League,” Rice said. “I picked the ball up on the edge of our own box and ran the whole pitch. As I was running forward I just remember the stadium getting louder and louder and louder, and as I scored the goal the place just erupted.

So I think that was one of my best solo goals.”

How fast and how much things have changed in the ensuing three years.  The only comfort for West Ham fans is that they can change back again just as fast. COYI!

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/personify-health-launches-updated-wellbeing-platform-experience-2-new-capabilities" hreflang="en">Personify Health launches updated wellbeing platform experience with 2 new capabilities</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →
Personify Health launches updated wellbeing platform experience with 2 new capabilities" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Personalized health platform Personify Health launched Tuesday a new wellbeing platform experience aimed at improving health outcomes and lowering costs for employers.

The updated platform experience features two new capabilities: adherence and digital navigation. The adherence tool is designed to keep members engaged and follow through on care plans, which the company says will help close gaps between finding the right care and taking action.

Meanwhile, digital navigation is an artificial intelligence-powered assistant delivered through the company’s member companion, Percy. The tool can answer coverage questions and is able to direct members to care within plans.

Personify Health, which was born in 2024 out of a merger between Virgin Pulse and HealthComp, serves over 30 million adults across thousands of U.S. employers, executives say.

Employers can add one or both capabilities with Personify’s core engagement experience where each member gets a Personalized Wellness Plan, the company says.

Personify Health has a planned go-live for these capabilities in 2027, according to Ed Liebowitz, Personify Health president and GM of wellness.

“We’re not just rethinking Personify Health with this launch,” Liebowitz said. “We’re changing how healthy happens, in our platform and across the wellbeing category. That means acknowledging where wellbeing should be extending well beyond the category’s current focus on engagement and proving value in terms of both outcomes and cost—where it matters most.”

Liebowitz told Fierce Healthcare that 2027’s expected rising healthcare costs for employers was one of the drivers behind the launch.

“I talk to a lot of benefits leaders who say they’d reposition their entire benefits strategy if someone could help them reverse that trend,” he said. “At the same time, they’re paying for benefits and programs that often go underused, and they’re under growing pressure from their CFOs to show what every program returns heading into renewals.”

Liebowitz noted cardiovascular disease, diabetes, high blood pressure and MSK are some of the “biggest cost drivers for employers,” adding programs “usually sit isolated from one another,” which ultimately leaves it up to “employees to figure out how to navigate their benefits.”

“Our platform stays with employees through that entire journey, from the moment a health risk shows up, ensuring they get the right care, and helping members stick with their care plan,” Liebowitz said. “Because we already have the context of each member’s health risks, we can connect them to the program that fits, and then that program becomes part of the personalized plan they’re already using with us.”

Interlull musings and shiny distractions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

We’re two days into the final Interlull of the year and, as you might expect, there’s not a huge amount going on. The senior fixtures don’t start until Thursday, so we haven’t even got a fresh batch of international press conference quotes to sink our teeth into. Slim pickings.

This time around, 20 of our 25 first-team players have been selected, which is a pretty remarkable tally. All the more so when you consider Noni Madueke can count himself unlucky to have missed out for England, while Bukayo Saka and William Saliba would ordinarily be shoo-ins.

Any concerns about the Frenchman’s absence against Hull, having travelled to Prague in midweek, were eased the following morning when he lined up for the under-21s against Aston Villa in Premier League 2. More minutes in his legs, rather than another injury to worry about.

Bolstered by Saliba and Max Dowman, whose domestic suspension kept him out of Saturday’s game, the youngsters ran out 5-2 winners. Given they’re currently 21st out of 31 teams in their Swiss-style league table, perhaps this is the way forward. Send the big guns down a level and start battering our way towards more club silverware. Relentless madman Arteta, finding new frontiers for his evil genius. He doesn’t just leave his technical area. He thinks outside it.

If you missed it, Max has been promoted to England’s under-21s for this window. He’ll join Arsenal loanees Tommy Setford and Ethan Nwaneri, as well as former Hale Enders Brooke Norton-Cuffy, who came off the bench for Hull on Saturday, and Amario Cozier-Duberry, who seems to be doing well at Middlesbrough on loan from Brighton.

Given Max has been in and around Mikel Arteta’s first team for a couple of seasons now, it’s easy to take milestones like this for granted. But playing under-21 international football at 16 is another significant step, even for someone whose talent has made rapid progress seem almost routine.

Back in September, Lee Carsley described his eventual promotion as inevitable, while stressing that the timing had to be right. The point was to bring him into a squad where he could play and compete, rather than move him up simply because everyone expected it.

Carsley referenced Nwaneri when discussing the temptation to push exceptional youngsters through the age groups. His point was that the aim is a long senior career, not winning a race to the first cap. That seems sensible enough. Max doesn’t need every appearance to become an audition for the next level.

A couple of games against Portugal and France should be a useful challenge in their own right. Carsley and Arteta also know each other from their Everton days, which ought to help when discussing what he needs.

With England, Scotland, Wales and the Republic of Ireland hosting Euro 2028, you can imagine that tournament already features somewhere in Max’s ambitions. There’s plenty of football between now and then, though. For the moment, let’s see how he gets on with this next step.

Elsewhere, most of our European internationals will be occupied with the Nations League, while the South American trio rack up the air miles again. Piero Hincapie heads home to Ecuador for friendlies against Costa Rica and Bolivia, while Gabriel and Bruno Guimaraes face Japan and Singapore, both in Singapore.

I appreciate Brazil’s options are more limited when European teams are tied up with UEFA fixtures, but their friendly schedule does sometimes feel like somebody has thrown a dart at a map. No doubt the commercial logic is compelling, but that doesn’t make you any less worried about tired hamstrings.

Anyway, fingers crossed they all come back with their limbs intact.

In other news, Italian publication Tuttosport has named Andrea Berta the winner of its Best Executive Award at this year’s Golden Boy Awards. The Arsenal sporting director collects the gong three transfer windows into his tenure, the latest of which ended with some fans calling for his head after the failed pursuits of Vinicius Junior and Julian Alvarez.

Either the bar is pretty low, he’s basking in the reflected glory of the team’s success, or it’s one of those shindigs where buying an inordinately expensive table guarantees you something shiny to take home. The cynic in me wasn’t exactly silenced by the news that Joan Laporta won Best President and Jorge Mendes Best Agent. In fact, I snorted when I read that. And lest we forget, Edu picked up a similar award from the same organisers in 2023…

Myles Lewis-Skelly was in contention for the actual Golden Boy award, which went to Barcelona defender Pau Cubarsi. Hard to argue too much with that, given the 19-year-old was at the heart of Spain’s World Cup win.

Cubarsi also made the FIFPRO World 11. More pertinently for us, so did David Raya. Having finished second to compatriot Unai Simon in the Ballon d’Or’s Best Goalkeeper category, I imagine there’s some satisfaction in getting the nod from the people who actually play the game. More than 26,000 professionals from 75 countries cast their votes, which is a fairly substantial dressing room to win over.

Alessia Russo, who is in fine form even if Arsenal Women are not, also earned her third selection for the Women’s World 11.

As you’ll have gathered from the snippiness above, I find it easy to be cynical about these things. There are so many awards now that you half expect to get one for finding your seat at the ceremony. Still, sometimes it’s nice to give people a pat on the back and recognise a job well done.

On an entirely unrelated note, have you voted for us to win Best Fan Media at the upcoming FSA Awards? You’ve only got a week left. A prestigious honour, that one. 😉

Right, I think that’s as much toothpaste as I can squeeze out of a slow news day. If anything else happens, we’ll have it covered on Arseblog News. Catch you next time.

Opinion: Liverpool must beat Man United to transfer of defender who has 'no fear'

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Liverpool and Manchester United have both been linked with a lot of the same players recently.

Each club needs to improve their ranks in the same sort of areas among their squads and, to do this, they're likely to clash heads every now and then.

But, recently, they seem to have been linked with the same players on multiple occasions.

Not only is this bad for both teams as their interest could drive the price in the players that they want up but, it could also cause them to lose some of their preferred targets to their rivals.

That's why almost every day, fans are seeing new players linked with an Anfield switch.

And, although most of these deals won't end up coming to anything, there's one player who the Reds have just been linked with that they should do everything in their power to sign.

Liverpool are in a race with Manchester United and Chelsea for Koni De Winter

Liverpool's defence has caused some serious concern this season.

Although some players such as Virgil van Dijk and Jeremy Jacquet have performed fairly well, other members of their back line have not met expectations.

This underperformance has led to many new defenders being linked with a Liverpool move.

However, a fresh update from Caught Offside may have just brought forward one of the most exciting players that Liverpool have been linked with yet.

"Liverpool, Manchester United, and Chelsea are all among the main suitors for one of the rising talents in Serie A this season," they wrote.

"The player in question is AC Milan defender Koni De Winter, who is growing in stature with almost every appearance at the moment, with Premier League scouts closely monitoring him in recent matches

"The 24-year-old only joined Milan last season and is not understood to be desperate for a move away, though he’s expected to be one to watch in upcoming transfer windows.

Everyone knows that before Araujo was signed by Liverpool, right-back was a huge problem for them.

And, with all the injuries their centre-backs suffered last term, Arne Slot's defence was massively hindered.

This led to Araujo being surprisingly signed but, since he's only on a season-long loan deal, it's unclear whether his transfer will be made permanent or not.

If it isn't, a new defender will still need to be signed and, since De Winter is only 24 years old and has already featured in 211 games across his career thus far, he's already got plenty of experience under his belt.

During the last couple of seasons, the Belgian hasn't been used as a right-back but, when playing for Juventus earlier in his career, he was used there regularly.

This past experience coupled with his ability to play at right-back are two of the main reasons why a deal for De Winter could be perfect for Liverpool.

He's come on leaps and bounds during his time with AC Milan and, if he was given a regular role by Liverpool, he'd only get better.

This, when Leonardo Bonucci has already claimed that he has "no fear" all join together to make him a perfect transfer target.

10 Best Winter Hiking Boots

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Today’s winter hiking boots are waterproof, insulated, and compatible with traction aids such as microspikes and snowshoes. While 200-gram insulated mids are fine for commuting and half-day hikes down to about 10-20 degrees (F), we recommend buying boots with higher calf coverage and 400-gram insulation for all-day hikes and mountain climbs in colder temperatures. We are experienced winter hikers with hundreds of successful mountain summits, and we understand what it takes to stay safe and comfortable on long, challenging winter hikes.

Here are our top 10 winter hiking boot recommendations:

You can’t use your regular 3-season boots or shoes for winter hiking because they lack waterproofing and insulation. Most insulated winter hiking boots are guaranteed waterproof out of the box. Many have rubber or waterproof synthetic soles, so you don’t have to worry about water soaking into the fabric. If moisture does get into your boots, insulated winter boots will still keep your feet warm. The same isn’t true of leather hiking boots, even when treated with waterproofing creams and sprays. If your regular hiking boots absorb water in winter, they can freeze and cause frostbite.

Winter boots should be waterproof and insulated to keep your feet dry and warm, have laces for a secure fit, be comfortable enough to wear all day, and be compatible with traction aids like microspikes and snowshoes. While the insulation you need varies, winter boots with non-removable synthetic insulation are the lightest and therefore the easiest to hike in. Synthetic boots are also lighter and more waterproof than leather boots.

Some boot manufacturers provide temperature ratings, but there’s no standard way to measure how warm winter boots are. Understand that these ratings are estimates at best and that users will have varying experiences based on their activity level, health, weight, sex, metabolism, and other factors.

Most 400 g insulated winter hiking boots suit all-day winter hikes and snowshoeing in subzero temperatures. They have calf-high uppers, which makes them warmer than mids because more of your lower leg is covered by insulation. Many manufacturers claim they’ll keep your feet warm down to -40F, but that assumes you’re actively hiking, not sitting around doing nothing. Realistically, they work best in temperatures from 30F down to -15F.  If you plan to climb 4000-footers in winter in New Hampshire’s White Mountains, the Adirondacks, Maine, or Vermont, we recommend using 400 g boots.

Most 200 g winter hiking boots are ankle-high mids and retain less heat on the lower leg than calf-height 400g boots. On the other hand, they’re a little easier to hike in because they’re lighter and feel more like regular hiking boots. 200 g boots suit temperatures from 30°F to 10°F and are ideal for milder winter conditions and shorter hikes, where you’re less likely to get caught out after dark when temperatures drop.

Mountaineering boots have rigid soles that work with crampons that lack flexible center bars, for rugged mountains with steeper slopes or thick ice. You can hike in them, but it feels very different from a soft-soled 400g or 200g insulated winter hiking boot. Most insulated mountaineering boot manufacturers don’t rate their boots’ temperature ranges, so you have to rely on anecdotal word-of-mouth about how warm they are. If you hike across glaciers or go on trips led by mountaineering guides, it’s usually best to ask them which boots to bring. They may even rent you boots.

While you should take the manufacturer’s temperature ratings with a grain of salt, they are a good indicator of a boot’s relative warmth. It’s difficult to compare warmth between boots with different insulation, but knowing how much insulation a boot has can help you compare models from the same manufacturer.

This is like a regular boot tongue, except the sides are closed and sewn to the boot sides to prevent water from leaking in when you step in puddles.

In addition to providing more protection, a beefy toe cap won’t collapse when you wear microspikes or crampons.

Your boots’ soles should provide good traction on loose or packed snow. Look for boots with a deep tread, like a Vibram sole, though you’ll likely augment your boot with microspikes.

Look for boots with a soft cuff that closes off the gap between your leg and boot.

A gaiter ring is a small ring attached to the top of the boot that you can hook your gaiters onto. It’s not the end of the world if your boots don’t have one since you can still hook the gaiter to your boot laces.

Grimshaw Begins Rebuilding Former School Turned Community Center in Chinatown, New York City

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Grimshaw has begun the reconstruction of 70 Mulberry Street, a historic community center in Manhattan's Chinatown, originally built as Public School 23 in the late nineteenth century. The building later became home to several of the neighborhood's cultural and nonprofit organizations before a fire in January 2020 destroyed its upper three floors and displaced five tenants. Led by the New York City Department of Design and Construction (DDC), the project will preserve portions of the historic building while providing a renewed home for the displaced organizations.

Grimshaw has begun the reconstruction of 70 Mulberry Street, a historic community center in Manhattan's Chinatown, originally built as Public School 23 in the late nineteenth century. The building later became home to several of the neighborhood's cultural and nonprofit organizations before a fire in January 2020 destroyed its upper three floors and displaced five tenants. Led by the New York City Department of Design and Construction (DDC), the project will preserve portions of the historic building while providing a renewed home for the displaced organizations.

The 85,720-square-foot reconstruction will return approximately 51,000 square feet to the five former tenants: the Chinese-American Planning Council, Chen Dance Center, Museum of Chinese in America, United East Athletics Association, and Chinatown Manpower Project. Alongside these spaces, the design by Grimshaw introduces 16,500 square feet of additional community facilities. The historic street-facing facades will be retained, maintaining the building's presence along Mulberry Street, while a double-height multipurpose room will accommodate recreation and community events. Meeting rooms, offices, and flexible spaces available for reservation and shared use will further support the organizations and the wider neighborhood.

The 85,720-square-foot reconstruction will return approximately 51,000 square feet to the five former tenants: the Chinese-American Planning Council, Chen Dance Center, Museum of Chinese in America, United East Athletics Association, and Chinatown Manpower Project. Alongside these spaces, the design by Grimshaw introduces 16,500 square feet of additional community facilities. The historic street-facing facades will be retained, maintaining the building's presence along Mulberry Street, while a double-height multipurpose room will accommodate recreation and community events. Meeting rooms, offices, and flexible spaces available for reservation and shared use will further support the organizations and the wider neighborhood.

The expanded facilities will also incorporate accessibility and environmental improvements. Three new elevators will provide access throughout the building, while operable windows will allow for natural ventilation. Landscaped terraces and street trees are intended to help mitigate the urban heat island effect, complementing the project's broader improvements to environmental performance. The reconstruction follows a community engagement process involving former tenants and neighborhood representatives, whose feedback informed the design. A permanent public artwork by Jennifer Wen Ma, commissioned through the NYC Department of Cultural Affairs' Percent for Art program, will also be incorporated into the building. The project will restore the organizations' shared home while expanding the facilities available for cultural, recreational, and community activities in Chinatown.

Hotel Chains’ Middle East Recovery Stalls as Peak Season Arrives

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Raffles Doha, a luxury hotel, is within the Katara Towers, inspired by the crossed swords of Qatar. Accor

The Middle East is a small slice of most hotel chains’ revenue but a bigger part of their pipelines. So far, they’re holding to their development plans, even as a second weak peak season looms.

The world’s largest hotel groups saw their Middle East business recover in the third quarter from the collapse that followed the outbreak of the U.S.-Iran war. But most now expect the region to weaken again in the fourth quarter, its most important season of the year, as Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabia prolong the uncertainty — and event cancellations there weigh on demand.

Accor, the France-headquartered hotels giant, has the most exposure to the region compared with its peers. The Middle East accounts for about 11% of its global room revenue, with Saudi Arabia at about 5% and the UAE at roughly 4%.

Its Middle East RevPAR fell 3% in the third quarter. “Saudi and Egypt continued to perform well, and the UAE RevPAR improved materially from the second quarter,” said Martine Gerow, group CFO, on an October 22 earnings call. UAE RevPAR was down by just over 20% and occupancy was back in the mid-60s percent, she said.

The world's largest hotel groups reported a Middle East recovery in the third quarter from the collapse that followed the outbreak of the U.S.-Iran war, but most now guide to renewed weakness in the fourth quarter — the region's peak season. Accor, with about 11% of global room revenue in the region, saw Middle East RevPAR fall 3% in Q3 and assumes a roughly 12% decline in Q4, with CFO Martine Gerow saying the company has taken a tougher view on Saudi Arabia as Houthi attacks intensify on Riyadh. Marriott's Middle East RevPAR fell 12% in Q3, an improvement from a drop of more than 40% in Q2, and the company expects a steeper fourth-quarter decline against tough comparisons. Hilton's Middle East and Africa RevPAR fell 5.6% and the company now expects a low double-digit decline for the region for the full year, with CEO Chris Nassetta saying owned hotels there will cut more than $20 million from earnings this year. IHG was broadly flat across the wider region but down 12% across the six Gulf countries, and Hyatt held RevPAR down only about 3% as domestic travel offset a 40% drop in international inbound. Development pipelines, meanwhile, are holding: IHG said it is aware of no project cancellations, and Wyndham recently signed five new contracts across Saudi Arabia and the UAE.

In OpenAI’s Ad Business, Advertisers Come Third

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ads in ChatGPT, it turns out, are a humanitarian effort.

Right at the top of OpenAI’s charter, the company states that its main mission is to bring the benefits of artificial general intelligence (AGI) – meaning AI that can match or outdo people at intellectual tasks – to all of humanity.

“Asterisk, without killing us,” quipped LUMA Partners CEO Terry Kawaja during an on-stage chat with Benji Shomair, OpenAI’s VP of monetization, at LUMA’s Digital Media Summit in New York City on Monday.

But to achieve this lofty goal depends on getting far more people to use ChatGPT than will ever pay for it.

In other words, Shomair said, for OpenAI to fulfil its mission “actually requires [us] to build an ad business.”

“We believe this technology is so beneficial, so incremental, so powerful,” he said, “that we need to create economic means to make it available to everyone.”

But putting ads inside a product that people confide in is a very delicate exercise.

“In my experience, ads or commercial messages haven’t ever been introduced into as intimate an environment as ChatGPT,” Shomair said. “Just think about the things that people are sharing with ChatGPT.”

And so OpenAI is building its ads business around three priorities, in this order: user trust, user value and, last, advertiser value. If you get the first two right, the third will follow, he said.

Which is all well and good as a sentiment. But what do you do when someone asks you to push the boundaries? Which does happen.

Since launching its advertising business less than a year ago, OpenAI has onboarded more than 50 ad tech, measurement and agency partners. “We’re taking a partner-first approach,” Shomair said. “We can’t do it all ourselves; it’s pragmatic.”

Sometimes, however, partners have a different idea of what’s fair game, and OpenAI just won’t go there, Shomair said.

“There are places where I think our partners would like us to do certain things that we just can’t,” he said, “and that’s because of how we think about making decisions.”

The decisions that OpenAI makes when it comes to its advertising business are guided by five principles: mission alignment (the whole bring-AGI-to-the-masses thing), answer independence, conversation privacy, choice and control and prioritizing user trust and user experience over revenue.

OpenAI devised its principles before it even started testing ads internally, and they still shape every product decision, Shomair said.

“We reference these principles on a pretty consistent basis,” he said, “and we use this as a way to communicate to our audiences how we’re thinking about trust.”

Even so, ads in AI chats are an easy punchline. Anthropic’s Super Bowl spots for Claude earlier this year gleefully poked fun at the very idea.

In one, a scrawny guy asks a buff trainer how to get a six-pack fast, in response to which the trainer starts hawking height-boosting insoles. In another, a man asks his therapist for advice on how to communicate better with his mom, and the therapist starts plugging a dating site that connects “sensitive cubs” with “roaring cougars.”

Those Anthropic ads had an impact. Claude’s daily download count went from roughly 63,000 in January to a peak of about 225,000 in mid-February, according to Appfigures, and its daily active users rose 11% after the game, per BNP Paribas.

Shomair acknowledged that the ads were funny. But they don’t reflect OpenAI’s approach to monetization in ChatGPT, he said, where the answers “are separate from the ads and not influenced by the ads.”

Which raises the question of what an ad even is or should be in a product like ChatGPT.

Shomair, who helped build Meta’s ads business before joining OpenAI last December, said part of the fun of his job is that new modes of consumer engagement force him to think about ads differently.

What, for instance, should advertising look like when consumers talk to a brand’s AI agent? Or when their own AI agent does the shopping for them? Or when two agents are just talking to each other?

Shomair said he doesn’t presume to know the answer. Even people working on the bleeding edge, the ones developing these things, are just experimenting, he said.

“It’s hard to forecast beyond six to 12 months,” he said. “The products I was working with 12 months ago versus the products I’m working on now – it’s like night and day.”

But there’s one thing he’s sure of: Nobody should sit on the sidelines. His advice to advertisers: “You’ve got to get your hands dirty,” he said. “You’ve got to jump in and play with it.”

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

Viant is doubling down on CTV with its $20 million Waymark acquisition, betting that AI-generated ads can bring more advertisers to streaming TV.

TIME is using AI agents to draft proposals and troubleshoot campaigns – but not to replace the human relationships that drive ad sales.

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Ultimate 1-50: the male footballers best associated with each squad number

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Who is football’s most legendary No 9? Has there ever been a legendary No 28? And why exactly does Mario Balotelli love wearing No 45 so much? Here’s our list and the stories behind it

The relationship between players and numbers is a long-established one filled with memories and fascinating as well as fun stories behind them. With that in mind, the Guardian has had a go at deciding which male player is best associated with each squad number, from 1-50. Various factors were taken into consideration, including how long a player wore/has worn that number, what they achieved/have achieved in it, why they decided to wear it in the first place and, in some cases, how distinctly it has become associated with them in death. The below list is highly subjective and ultimately a bit of fun, but don’t let that stop you being shocked and furious in equal measure.

One is the loneliest number, but it also allows you to stand out – especially in football, where you play by different rules and wear different attire. Nobody stood out quite like Yashin, the almost mythical USSR and Dinamo Moscow goalkeeper of the 1950s and 1960s. He often wore a flat cap in goal, and his trademark kit led to the nicknames “Black Spider” or “Black Panther” (strictly speaking, it should have been the less catchy Very Very Dark Blue Spider). Yashin remains the only goalkeeper to have won the Ballon d’Or; with a respectful nod to Dino Zoff, Peter Schmeichel and others, he was a simple choice.

It would be a crime against dynamism and flair if we didn’t give one of the traditional full-back numbers to a Brazilian. Some of their great right-backs didn’t actually wear No 2 – Djalma Santos and Carlos Alberto, World Cup-winners of 1958 and 1970, sported No 4 – but that still leaves us with the richest pickings. It’s hard to look beyond Cafu, a bullet train on the right for Brazil, Roma, Milan and others. He won two World Cups and is the only man to play in three consecutive finals. Chuck in more than 700 club games, and that’s a mind-boggling amount of runs up that right flank.

Few could dispute this choice. Maldini is No 3. Milan retired his number – it can only be used again if one of his sons plays for the club – and it’s almost a surprise Fifa didn’t do the same. As a left-back or left-sided centre-back, Maldini came as close as anyone to perfecting defending; on the ultra-rare occasions he was given a chasing, by Chris Waddle or Karel Poborsky, it was a JFK moment. It’s an oddity that he didn’t wear No 3 at Italia 90 or USA 94. But for most of his career, to see Maldini with any other number would have been like seeing James Bond in a shell suit.

For longevity and sheer class – on and off the field – Zanetti pips Pep Guardiola, Sergio Ramos and the aforementioned Brazilian right-backs to this number. A Swiss Army knife of a footballer, Zanetti was one of the first players to show that the positions of right-back and centre-midfield spoke the same language. He won 16 trophies in 19 years at Inter, 15 of them as a quietly inspirational captain. José Mourinho said “it was an honour to coach him”, while Maldini called him “my most respected enemy”. As was the case with Maldini, Zanetti’s number was retired by a Milanese giant.

With apologies to Zinedine Zidane, Fabio Cannavaro and Obdulio Varela, the terrifying and granite-willed captain of Uruguay’s 1950 World Cup winners, this was never in doubt. Beckenbauer, libero and captain of the all-conquering West Germany and Bayern Munich sides of the 1970s, was born to stroll around a football field, No 5 on his back, an imaginary Andrex-soft sax jazz soundtrack playing in the background. His biographer, Uli Hesse, wrote that the number “became synonymous with his unique interpretation of the libero role”. Nobody did it better; nobody ever will, which is essentially why, quite rightly, Bayern retired their No 5 shirt in December 2024.

Iconography trumps tactics on this one. In modern football, a No 6 is a deep-lying midfielder, but in the 20th century it was indelibly associated with a specific genre of centre-back: captains, leaders, legends. The list is headed by Bobby Moore, Tony Adams and the don; Baresi, a manager on the field for Arrigo Sacchi and Fabio Capello during Milan’s golden age, his shirt retired by the club in 1997. Baresi died this year; if you have any doubts that he is the definitive No 6, just look at the pictures from his funeral in August of this year.

This probably should have been given to Garrincha, who wore No 7 when he single-footedly won the 1962 World Cup for Brazil, but you can’t argue with a trademark. Never mind the trifling matter of almost 1,000 career goals, CR7 is a brand that includes fragrances, underwear, footwear, lifestyle hotels, a fitness app … even bottled water. It all started in 2003, when Sir Alex Ferguson encouraged the teenager from Portugal to embrace one of the most iconic shirts in football – Manchester United No 7s include George Best, Bryan Robson, Eric Cantona and David Beckham. On a global scale, Ronaldo, now retired from international duty, is the most famous of the lot.

Desailly embraced the No 8 shirt when he was repurposed as a central midfielder at Milan then wore it when he played centre-half for the France team that won France 98 and Euro 2000. Desailly won the lot as a No 8, with the enduring memory of him in that number coming from the 1994 Champions League final, as the then 25-year-old rampaged through Barcelona to score the final goal. Xavi, for Spain, Andrés Iniesta, for Barcelona, and Socrates were strong shouts, all more traditional No 8s, but Desailly got the nod.

A choice that was painfully obvious, romantic and a bit dispiriting. Let’s start with the good stuff: Ronaldo 1.0 was the greatest young player we’ve ever seen, blessed like nobody before or since with the holy trinity of power, speed and skill. The dispiriting bit is that his Nike Mercurial R9 boots, unveiled in the buildup to France 98, catalysed an age of naked commercialism that football has never escaped. (Paradoxically, Ronaldo was wearing No 10 for Inter at the time, though that would soon change.) Let’s just remember the good stuff – R9, the first PlayStation footballer, and the platonic ideal of a distinctly Brazilian brilliance.

“No matter what happens and no matter who is in charge, the No 10 shirt will always be mine.” We’re not going to argue with Maradona, even if Lionel Messi, Pelé or Ferenc Puskas were the most compelling alternatives. To Maradona, the No 10 was a way of life, part of a uniquely Argentinian identity – that of the pibe, the creative genius from a poor background blessed with celestial skill and the vicious defiance of an alley cat. He even had the No 10 tattooed on his back. Close your eyes and you can probably see the font.

“I remember the first time I saw him,” said Sir Alex Ferguson. “He was 13 and just floated over the ground like a cocker spaniel chasing a piece of silver paper in the wind.” Giggs wore a lot of different numbers at the start of his career – 2, 4, 5, 7, 10 – but from the moment the Premier League introduced squad numbers in 1993, he was Manchester United’s No 11, even when he changed position in his dotage. There were other 24-carat contenders, including the Brazilian collective of Garrincha, Rivellino, Eder, Romario, Rivaldo and Ronaldinho, but none can match Giggs’s longevity or medal collection.

After an injury-ruined season with Milan, Van Basten started Euro 88 on the bench, both literally and numerically. He came in for the second game against England, scored a hat-trick and never looked back. A Hitchcockian late winner against the hosts, West Germany, was followed by an entirely preposterous volley in the final against USSR. From then on he was the Netherlands’ No 9. But the legend of No 12 endured, particularly when Thierry Henry was playing for France: he chose No 12 because of Van Basten. Were it not for the Dutchman, another Frenchman, the great Alain Giresse, would have been our choice.

The German sense of humour has always been underrated: they, more than any other country, have embraced the No 13 shirt. It’s been worn at major tournaments by Rudi Völler, Karl-Heinz Riedle, Michael Ballack, Thomas Müller – and Gerd Müller. He picked it for the 1970 and 1974 World Cups, and a triumphant Euro 72, as a nod to his childhood hero, Max Morlock, who scored in the 1954 World Cup final win over Hungary. Muller played 13 World Cup games and almost finished with 13 goals. The alternative ending was better: his 14th, in the 1974 final against the Netherlands, won the World Cup.

Cruyff’s first encounter with No 14 came by chance. Ajax’s Gerrie Muhren couldn’t find his usual No 7 shirt, so Cruyff gave him the No 9 and pulled the first spare shirt out of the bag. Ajax won, Cruyff kept wearing No 14 and embraced every connection: for example, he’d married his wife, Danny, on 2 December 1968 and pointed out that 2+12 and 6+8 both equal 14. The Cruyff Foundation later set up Schoolyard14, the key principle of which is the 14 Rules of Johan Cruyff. If he were a young footballer in 2026, Cruyff would be the first to have a symbol instead of a number.

This was the toughest of the teens, with nobody demanding selection. We plumped for a man who, as far as we can tell, spent only five games in the No 15 shirt. Falcão, an imperious box-to-box playmaker, missed all Brazil’s World Cup qualifiers for Spain 82 – Roma didn’t have to release him, so they didn’t – and as a result was not among Nos 1-11. But he played all five games at the tournament in the No 15 shirt and won the Silver Ball, which makes him officially the best player in the greatest attacking team many of us have ever seen.

Sham! Yes, it’s somewhat awkward having a certain era of Manchester City players on this list given “events” a few weeks ago, but, equally, it’s impossible to ignore them entirely given their achievements on the pitch, and that’s very much the case when it comes to Rodri, a man who completed football by the age of 30. He’s won the World Cup, the Euros, the Ballon d’Or, the Golden Ball, the Treble … the lot. When he moved to Barcelona in the summer, Rodri asked Fermin López if he could take the No 16 shirt that has served him so well. We assume it was a short conversation.

It’s all because of Wilfried Bony, you know. Bony was wearing No 14, the number Kevin De Bruyne wore at Wolfsburg, when De Bruyne moved to Manchester City in 2015. He picked No 17 instead. It’s apt there’s a “7” in there somewhere, such was De Bruyne’s devastating impact in the inside-right channel during his decade at the Etihad. His creative genius made him an irresistible choice, despite the appeal of Emmanuel Petit, Frank Rijkaard, Vitinha and even a young Cristiano Ronaldo. For a fair chunk of his time in England, De Bruyne was the best player in the best league in the world.

If you’re going to launch the toys, you might as well do it in style. When Ronaldo took the No 9 shirt at Inter in the summer of 1998 – the Brazilian had joined the club 12 months earlier and initially wore No 10 – the incumbent had an idea. Ivan Zamorano had worn No 9 for Chile, Sevilla, Real Madrid and Inter and took enormous pride in the symbolism of the shirt. So he switched to No 18 and had a plus sign stitched into the shirt: 1+8. Long before Lionel Messi, this was a different kind of false nine.

The Golden Ball winner at Italia 90 wore No 19. But enough about Toto Schillaci. Gascoigne’s unfettered, painfully innocent brilliance drove Bobby Robson’s team to the semi-finals of a tournament they were expected to stink out, and ultimately changed the game in his country. In 2009, England and West Germany met in a charity-match repeat of the 1990 semi-final. England wore Nos 1-11 – except Gascoigne, who had No 19 on his back. Robson, who died five days later, attended the match in a wheelchair. On the way home, he had one pressing question for his son: “How did Gascoigne play?”

Paolo Rossi, Clarence Seedorf and Ole Gunnar Solskjær are among those who have worn No 20 with great distinction but no one is, and probably ever will be, as synonymous with it as Jota. It was referenced in the song Liverpool fans sung about him with great lustre when he was alive and with great poignancy after he died, with the club’s retirement of the number and building of a ‘Forever 20’ memorial outside Anfield further tying the late Portuguese striker to the digits. Gone far too soon, Jota will never be forgotten.

The Italian was always a mature vintage, a smooth midfielder who could spot a pass from any angle. He could have played many of his games with a glass of wine in his hand and when he had his own vineyard, 21 Brut Rose was produced by Pratum Coller in honour of the great man’s squad number. No 21 adorned Pirlo’s back at Inter, Milan, Juventus and New York City, as he collected six Serie A titles and a couple of Champions League winner’s medals, not to mention a World Cup with Italy in 2006.

The 2007-08 Ballon d’Or was an inevitability for the magical Brazilian, who spent the year running riot with his third-choice of two little ducks. The attacking midfielder wanted No 8 or No 10 when he arrived in Milan but Gennaro Gattuso and Rui Costa were already in possession of those respective when he joined from São Paulo and the youngster was not going to mess with two legends. His time at Real Madrid, after a €67m move, was far less successful and Kaká returned to Milan after four years, taking back No 22 in the process.

With Raul wearing No 7, Beckham needed a plan upon joining Real Madrid in July 2003. He turned to Michael Jordan for inspiration, asking for his iconic digits as he became a galáctico. When Beckham moved to LA Galaxy four years later, he stuck with No 23. “The connection with Jordan and how important the No 23 was, I just felt it was the right thing to do,” Beckham said. Sol Campbell was another strong contender for No 23 given he wore it at Tottenham and Arsenal, as was Marc-Vivien Foé, whose No 23 shirt was retired by Manchester City after his shock death in June 2003.

“I really hope to stay in the heart of this city so that one day, Napoli also retire the No 24,” Insigne said. The Naples-born forward spent 16 years with his boyhood club, which is certainly a good way to ingratiate himself with the fans, some of whom wanted him to take Maradona’s iconic No 10. “Maradona’s 10? I’m very superstitious, plus I wear 24 because it’s my wife’s birthday and it’s brought me luck,” Insigne said.

It says something about a player’s association with a number that his blessing is required before it can be passed on to someone else. And so it was in August 2023 that Zola gave Moisés Caicedo the go ahead to be Chelsea’s No 25, with no else deemed worthy of taking that status from the Italian for two whole decades. A bit much? Not really given the brilliance of Zola’s performances across seven seasons at the club. There was the skill, the goals, and that sense of joy that made him popular far beyond the boundaries of west London. Quite simply, everybody loved to watch Franco play.

Many academy graduates are desperate to rid themselves of their first squad number once they have settled into the first team but the Chelsea centre-back stuck with what he was given for 18 years. One of the reasons Terry did so was because he saw supporters getting it printed on their own shirts and did not want them to buy another. What a nice captain, leader, legend, and indeed why fix something that isn’t broke? When a mural of Terry and Antonio Conte was painted on to Stamford Bridge, the “26” was far more prominent than his face, which really does say something.

It was at Sampdoria where the No 27 became imprinted on Quagliarella’s back, and in tribute to his former teammate Niccolo Galli, who died in a road accident aged 17. “I have no doubt that had he lived, he would have been captain of Arsenal and of Italy,” Arsène Wenger said of Galli, who he managed in north London before his passing in February 2001. Quagliarella continued to honour Galli throughout his career, taking in moves to Udinese, Napoli and Juventus, every time ensuring he got the number he wanted. At Juve he spent two seasons with No 18, but the forward’s cherished number was with him for two Serie A titles.

History shows that No 28 is often discarded by those seeking fame and fortune – Cristiano Ronaldo had it at Sporting as a kid, Steven Gerrard likewise at Liverpool – but Touré wore it for four seasons at Arsenal, a period that saw him become an Invincible. That perhaps explains why, after a three-season dalliance with No 5, he reverted to what he knew upon joining Manchester City in July 2009, going on to win another Premier League title with this most random of numbers on his back. Quite clearly it meant a lot to him, and justifiably so.

“I have had it now for many years,” Havertz said of the number he’s worn at Bayer Leverkusen, Chelsea and Arsenal. “When I became a professional, I took it because it has some history with my brother – we used to take it always when we played video games together. Since then I just took it, and for me it’s the perfect number. I love to wear it in a red shirt again.” It’s as good a reason as any, to be honest, and given Havertz’s success in No 29, why he gets the nod over some other contenders, including Monaco-era Kylian Mbappé.

Sometimes it only takes a goal to etch a player into a club’s history, and when that goal is genuinely seismic, the number he wore at the time also takes on a grand status. And so it is that Grønkjær is our No 30 given that’s what the Danish winger had on when he scored Chelsea’s winner against Liverpool on the final day of the 2002-03 season. Chelsea subsequently secured Champions League football over Gérard Houllier’s side, which convinced Roman Abramovich to buy the club that summer and change Chelsea’s fortunes, as well as the landscape of modern football, for ever.

Upon signing for Manchester United in July 2015, Schweinsteiger was given the No 23 shirt. He quickly changed to No 31 and, really, that was no surprise given that was the number he wore throughout his golden 13-year spell at Bayern Munich, coming through the ranks to win eight Bundesliga titles and the Champions League. The midfielder kept it during his two years at United and also wore it during his two years at Chicago Fire. Quite simply Schweinsteiger is No 31 (albeit he mainly wore No 7 for Germany) and no one else comes close to challenging him on that.

This feels like a number that should have generated a lot of candidates but ultimately there weren’t many, and certainly no one as notable as Tevez. From the shock, awe and controversy of his three years at West Ham, to the rampaging success of his two-year spell at Manchester United, to the wild, wonderful and also controversial nature of his four years at Manchester City, Tevez well and truly made No 32 his own, going on to also wear it at Shanghai Shenhua and upon his return to Boca Juniors. With apologies to Joël Matip, but it wasn’t even close in the end.

This was an immediate lock-in given Cech’s reasons for taking this number upon joining Arsenal from Chelsea in 2015. He was 33 at the time, had played 333 games in the Premier League and – wait for it – 33 is number of vertebrae in the human spine. “Hope to be a part of the spine of the team,” Cech posted on social media on 11 July 2015. Seriously, what a guy. Speaking of Arsenal, Riccardo Calafiori was also in contention given he wore No 33 at Basel and Bologna before nabbing it upon his arrival at the Emirates two years ago.

The choice for this number came down to Nigel de Jong or Xhaka. The former wore it throughout his five seasons at Manchester City, four at Milan, one at LA Galaxy and one at Galatasaray. Strong but not strong enough given Xhaka wore it during his three seasons at Basel, four at Borussia Monchengladbach, five of his seven at Arsenal, two at Leverkusen and throughout his time so far at Sunderland. And here’s the clincher: the Switzerland captain adopted No 34 in the first place because that was the number of the bus he took to training when in the youth ranks at Basel. It just had to be him.

A couple of Argentinians were in the running for this number, namely Pablo Aimar, who wore No 35 at different times for Valencia and River Plate, and Diego Placente, who wore it throughout his five seasons at Bayer Leverkusen, a period in which he played an important role in them finishing runners-up in the Bundesliga and Champions League. But ultimately this was no contest given Akinfeev’s remarkable association with No 35, the Russian goalkeeper taking it on upon graduating from CSKA Moscow’s youth ranks to the first-team squad in 2003 and, 23 years later, still retaining it. That, folks, is dedication to your squad number.

Martín Zubimendi was a strong candidate for this number given the reason he adopted it upon joining Arsenal in July 2025 was because he also wore it when making his debut for Real Sociedad, his boyhood club, six years earlier. But, in the end, longevity overruled sentiment in favour of Darmian, who wore No 36 across a 10-year spell for Milan, Padova, Palermo, Torino, Manchester United, Parma and Inter. There doesn’t appear to be any specific reason why the Italian full-back was so wedded to such a random number, but he was, and for a long time.

Van de Ven has only worn No 37 for the past four seasons, ever since joining Tottenham from Wolfsburg in August 2023. He didn’t wear it while at the German club or at Volendam, where the Dutch centre-back began his career. But his reason for doing so is why he’s the clear choice. “A friend of mine passed away when we were young and it was always his favourite number,” Van de Ven revealed in a February 2024 interview. “So when I had the opportunity to choose No 37 when I signed for Tottenham … to be honest, it’s for him.”

Born in Australia and raised in Macau, Tombides joined West Ham’s academy in 2008 as a promising teenage striker. He had talent and dedication and made his first-team debut in a League Cup tie against Wigan in September 2012. That game came a year after Tombides had been diagnosed with testicular cancer. He died from the disease in April 2014, aged only 20. Shortly after, West Ham retired his No 38 shirt. It was only the second time the club had done such a thing, the first being with Bobby Moore’s No 6 shirt, a sign of just how well thought of young Tombides was in east London.

Guimarães chose No 39 upon making his debut for Athletico Paranaense in 2017 and has worn it ever since, at Lyon, Newcastle and now Arsenal, because it was the dispatch number of the taxi his father drove in Rio de Janeiro. Quite clearly it means a lot to the Brazil midfielder, as he made clear in an article published by The Players’ Tribune in February 2023. “For me 39 is special. No, it’s more than that. It’s magical. It gave me everything in life”. Sorry Marcus Rashford in his first season at Manchester United, but you’re not topping that.

Idrizaj joined Swansea from German club Eilenburg in August 2009 as a promising 21-year-old striker. He went on to make three first-team appearances that season with plenty more seemingly on the horizon. But on 15 May 2010, Idrizaj died of a suspected heart attack while at home in his native Austria. It was a sad shock that led to Swansea retiring the former Liverpool player’s No 40 shirt. That showed how much everyone at the club thought of him, which was seen again when Swansea’s players wore T-shirts bearing Idrizaj’s name after winning the 2011 Championship playoff final.

Rice’s reason for wearing No 41 throughout his time in West Ham’s first team and ever since joining Arsenal in July 2023 is pretty straightforward. “It was a number I was given in the [West Ham] academy by the kitman, Jamo. It’s stayed with me since,” he said. “Regardless of what happens in my career, I might stay with 41. ‘Rice 41’ has a nice ring to it”. That’s debatable, but what’s for sure is that Rice has become distinctly associated with the number as he’s gone about establishing himself as one of the best midfielders in the world.

Touré chose No 42 upon joining Manchester City in July 2010 because it was the reverse of the No 24 he had worn at Barcelona. A simple if somewhat odd motivation that very much did not backfire as the Ivorian went about making the number his own during an eight-year spell at City that saw him win three Premier League titles, two FA Cups, two League Cups and become one of the most impactful, impressive midfielders on the planet. Touré retained No 42 upon joining Olympiakos in September 2018 before going back to 24 at his final club – Qingdao Huanghai in China.

“It was my first number in professional football, it’s a lucky number I guess”. Klich’s reason for wearing No 43 is, like with many others, based on what was given to him at the start of his career, which in Klich’s case occurred at Cracovia in his native Poland. He subsequently wore it for two seasons at PEC Zwolle, one at FC Twente, six at Leeds, two at DC United, one at Atlanta United and now back at Cracovia. Klich never wore No 43 at Wolfsburg, Kaiserslautern or Utrecht, so it clearly doesn’t mean that much to him, but he’s done enough to make it on this list.

Has a man ever loved No 4 as much as Perisic? It feels unlikely given he has worn various versions of it – namely “4”, “14” and “44” – throughout his career. He’s not No 4 on this list because that is Javier Zanetti, which is also the reason why Perisic couldn’t have No 4 upon joining Inter in August 2015 – the number had been retired by the club in honour of the Argentina great the previous year. Perisic subsequently took No 44, which the Croatian also wore at Club Brugge and Borussia Dortmund. That’ll do as far as this list is concerned, especially given his only real rival is Massimo Oddo.

Why [always] him? Well, it could’ve been Roméo Lavia or one or two others, but ultimately it had to be the man who wore No 45 with great fuss and furore at Inter, Manchester City and Liverpool before also doing so at Milan, Brescia, Monza, Adana Demirspor, FC Sion and Genoa. No 45 has become the number for one of the careers of the modern era and the association is only strengthened by its sweet origin story. As Balotelli explained: “When I first played with Inter, the numbers for young players were No 36 to No 50. I took No 45 because four plus five is nine – and I scored. It brings me luck.”

The Italian goalkeeper represented 12 different clubs in a 19-year career and only wore No 46 at one of them. But he gets the nod because of the nice, circular nature of the association. Sirigu wanted No 6 before making his Serie A debut for Palermo in 2009 but was told he couldn’t because it wasn’t appropriate for a keeper by the manager, and someone who knows a thing or two about shot-stopping, Walter Zenga. So he chose No 46 because it was the one worn by the legendary Italian motorcyclist Valentino Rossi. Sirigu left Palermo in 2011 before returning 13 years later. The number he chose upon doing so? 46.

This was a shoo-in for a couple of reasons: Foden has worn the number throughout his time as a first-team player at Manchester City, which is verging on a decade and seen him win everything going, and because of the origin story. Foden’s grandfather Ronnie, a big City fan, passed away when he was only 47. “I was young when we lost him,” said Foden in a 2024 interview. “My dad said he loved playing football with me and that it would be amazing if I took the shirt number. I have loved it ever since.” Foden’s tribute to Ronnie extends to having “47” tattooed behind his right ear.

There’s no point sugarcoating it – this is a stretch. Jones only wore No 48 for two years at Liverpool, from 2018-2020, and there appears no interesting reason for him doing so. But the midfielder gets the nod because the competition is razor-thin bordering on nonexistent. It certainly helps Jones’s cause that he wore No 48 when scoring the winner in Liverpool’s FA Cup third-round victory over Everton at Anfield in January 2020. It was a superb, long-range curling finish that made Jones, at 18 years and 340 days, Liverpool’s youngest goalscorer in a Merseyside derby since Robbie Fowler in March 1994.

“Forty-nine, forty-nine, Lewis-Skelly” sing Arsenal supporters in praise of their academy graduate as well as in recognition of why he has kept hold of such a random number even after establishing himself in Mikel Arteta’s first-team squad. Lewis-Skelly was given the choice of changing to a more established number ahead of last season but stuck with No 49 because of its association with Arsenal’s Invincibles-era unbeaten run of games, and an Arsenal song ever since (‘Forty-nine, forty-nine undefeated’). Sound reasoning and enough to earn the 20-year-old a place on this list ahead of Ronaldinho, who wore No 49 at Atlético Mineiro and Querétaro.

Another shoo-in given the unsurprisingly sparse options for this number and the fact Markovic has worn it for the bulk of his long, nomadic career. He first adopted it when he broke through at Partizan Belgrade in 2011 and continued to do so at Benfica and Liverpool, as well as at Fenerbahce, Hull and Anderlecht – who he joined on loan during his time at Anfield – before taking it again upon his permanent return to Partizan in 2019. The Serbian winger has since gone on to wear it at Gaziantep FK, Trabzonspor, FC Baniyas and Apollon Limassol. Markovic is No 8 at current club, FK Cukaricki, but clearly, at heart, he’s Mr No 50.

Who have we missed from our this ultimate 1-50 list? Tell us the male player (or players) we should have included and why. Feel free to let us know why they mean so much to your club or country. Let us know in the form below.
