Ethereum – Will ETH’s $432M liquidation shock put $2K support at risk?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

On the 8th and 9th of November, Ethereum [ETH] witnessed a combined liquidation of $432.34 million worth of leveraged positions. As a result, the ETH price slid from $2,236 to $2,060.

This was the largest wave of liquidations for Ethereum since the 19th of August, when $1.02 billion worth of short positions were taken out in a single day.

At the time of writing, Ethereum was trading just above $2.1k. Swing traders and investors would be keenly watching the $2,000 psychological level to assess if the next wave of selling was upon us.

In other news, Vitalik Buterin presented reasons why Ethereum as a store of value made “more sense to focus on” than ETH as a fee-burning token.

The 30-day rolling Ethereum Open Interest Z-Score fell to -2.87. Using data from Binance, crypto analyst Arab Chain presented data that revealed an unusually sharp decline in Open Interest (OI) behind ETH contracts.

The OI was at $5.236 billion, compared to the 30-day average of $5.850 billion, with a standard deviation of $213.77 million. This meant that the current OI was about 2.87 standard deviations below the 30-day mean.

Meanwhile, persistent selling pressure has pushed the leading altcoin’s price down towards $2,100. Declining OI and downward price pressure could be due to the closure of existing positions, through liquidations or manual exits.

It is not necessarily a sign that there were sizable short positions opened, the analyst wrote.

The ETH price structure was bearish after the loss of the swing point at $2,358. The $2,400 area has since been retested as a resistance zone, and the price has reacted bearishly from there in November.

The OBV was relatively flat over the past ten days, but it has been declining slowly since early October. The MACD was also declining and signaled strong bearish momentum at the time of writing.

The $2,000 round-number support was one level to watch. The $1,840-$1,950 area demand zone where ETH consolidated in July and August was also a place investors can wait for a bullish reaction from.

Introducing The Checkout, ADWEEK’s New Commerce Newsletter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Commerce is the ultimate driver of the marketing industry, and the economy. And everything about it is changing, from how people find products and services to how they actually execute the purchase.

ADWEEK’s deputy editor Lauren Johnson and senior reporter Mitchell Parton have already teamed up to bring you the biggest scoops and analysis on this extremely important topic. On Tuesday (Nov. 10) at 12pm ET, we’re launching The Checkout, a newsletter that will keep you up-to-date with all the commerce and retail news you need to know, while also providing the analysis to help you orient your own thinking.

From retail media to agentic commerce, Johnson and Parton will guide you through all of the new technologies and practices changing how we shop, and put it together into an easy-to-digest package of insights that will keep you in the know.

Because there’s a lot going on right now. Retailers, search platforms, AI companies, and social media platforms are all trying to own the commerce narrative. Some are even betting that agentic commerce is the way of the future. But while consumers have embraced AI for research and discovery, they’re still hesitant to give it control over transactions.

“The big question is how automated the shopping process will become, and whether consumers will bite,” Parton said.

The Checkout will also continue to dive into the maturation of retail media, which is still a major growth area. But, as more retailers invest, is there enough revenue to go around?

“Six years into the retail media boom, how are companies growing and justifying their ad businesses to be media companies?” Johnson said.

Gustaf Westman unveils full Disney collection featuring Donald Duck bookstand and Pluto mirror

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Swedish designer Gustaf Westman‘s full collaboration with media company Disney, which saw him reimagine familiar Disney details “through his own design language”, has been revealed.

The collection, which launches on 15 November, features homeware, accessories and glassware, alongside made-to-order furniture.

Among the furniture pieces are a bookstand designed to resemble Donald Duck, a Goofy coffee table and Mickey dresser, as well as a Pluto mirror.

Each takes recognisable features from the Disney characters and turns them into parts of the design object, with the Donald bookstand decorated with his hat and duck feet.

Westman told Dezeen he wanted to work with Disney because he grew up with the brand.

“Disney is part of so many people’s childhood, including mine,” the designer explained.

“Mickey’s ears and white gloves, Minnie’s bow, Goofy’s playful silhouette – they’re instantly recognisable shapes, and that made them really fun to work with,” he continued.

His aim was to create something new while using details that are deeply familiar to many.

“I wanted to take those familiar details and reimagine them through my own design language, with sculptural, playful forms in bold proportions, and bring that sense of joy into people’s homes,” Westman said.

The collection also includes smaller objects such as a vase designed to look like Mickey’s trousers, a glass that evokes Minnie Mouse and a candelabra resembling Donald Duck.

There is also merch, including a T-shirt, hoodie, keychains, hairbands and pins.

Westman’s own favourite object is the Mickey Tumbler, which was one of the first objects from the collection to be revealed.

“The Mickey Tumbler is my favourite,” he said.

“It combines my chunky shapes with Mickey’s signature colours and an ear-shaped lid, so both worlds are instantly recognisable without overcomplicating the design.”

Disney and Westman are hosting a series of pop-up experiences in Tokyo, New York and Paris to launch the collection before it is released online.

Disney also targeted the homeware and interior industries in 2022, when it launched its own homeware brand.

Minimal Maximum / Stempel & Tesar Architekti

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Text description provided by the architects. This minimalist family home is the architect's own house—a personal manifesto in which more than two decades of designing residential architecture for others is condensed into a single, highly concentrated response to a demanding urban plot in Prague. Every centimetre became a deliberate decision, resulting in a "minimal maximum"—a house without corridors, organised around a central dining space that rises four metres and connects three floors through a staggered half-level system.

Andrew McCarthy on the Hegseth Impeachment

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

A prominent conservative legal commentator explains why Hegseth should be convicted on at least some of the charges.

Yesterday, I wrote about why Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth deserves to be impeached and convicted for his role in in starting an unconstitutional war and perpetrating over 200 cases of outright murder with the "drug boat" strikes. Today GOP Rep. Thomas Massie filed impeachment charges in the House of Representatives. Even if they don't pass in the narrowly Republican-controlled lame duck Congress, they will almost certainly do so in the incoming Democratic one. Meanwhile prominent conservative legal commentator, national security law expert and former federal prosecutor Andrew McCarthy has published an excellent overview of the charges in the National Review.

McCarthy is far to my right, and far more sympathetic to the War on Drugs (I myself believe it should be abolished). Thus, it's notable that he and I agree that Hegseth should be convicted for his role in the drug boat strikes, which McCarthy characterizes as "indefensible":

I do not believe there is any viable justification for the extrajudicial killings by military force of operators of suspected drug boats. Drug trafficking is a crime, not an act of war. Our law addresses it through trial and imprisonment, not the use of military force…. The administration has not proved that the boats were ferrying narcotics, much less that the drugs were headed to the United States. The president's designation of Venezuelan criminal gangs as foreign terrorist organizations, besides being legally dubious (drug dealing is not "terrorist activity" as defined in federal law), does not authorize the use of force (it instead makes material support to the organization a crime and helps the government cut off funding sources). While the unauthorized killings in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific have not drawn the level of public ire that the Iran war has, they are indefensible, and Hegseth has been their loudest, crudest champion.

As noted in my earlier post, this view is similar to that of John Yoo, well-known former Bush Administration official and leading conservative advocate of sweeping executive power over national security and foreign affairs.

McCarthy also argues Hegseth should be convicted on at least one other charge:

Similarly, I do not believe there is any defending Hegseth on the charge that he has abused command authority and violated constitutional separation-of-powers principles in trying to discipline and censure Senator Kelly, a retired Navy captain (who remains subject to the Uniform Code of Military Justice). Hegseth's actions, undoubtedly endorsed by the president, constituted unlawful command influence. More importantly, they abused executive power in an effort to muffle congressional criticism. The haughty Hegseth was playing a dangerous game here; if it has now come back to bite him, he has himself to blame.

This charge is less within my expertise than some of the others. But I agree with McCarthy here.

McCarthy also appears to recognize that the Iran War is illegal, but suggests that Hegseth does not deserve impeachment on this score, because he was just obeying Trump's orders and implementing his policy. He also notes (correctly) that previous presidents have gotten away with similar initiations of war without congressional authorization.

I disagree for reasons summarized in my previous post:

Obviously, the ultimate perpetrator of these illegal acts is not Hegseth, but Trump. The former was following the latter's illegal orders (though he likely also helped influence Trump to give those orders in the first place). But sanctioning officials for obeying illegal orders is still important. That's one of the lessons of the Nuremberg trials.

I would add that Hegseth wasn't just some low-level flunky merely obeying orders. He has had a major influence on policy, and was one of the key architects of both the Iran War and the boat strikes. OK, maybe he is a flunky; but not a low-level one. He bears far greater responsibility than, e.g., servicemembers at the bottom of the chain of command.

As also noted in my earlier post, it is indeed true that previous presidents, such as Barack Obama, have gotten away with starting illegal wars. Obama deserved to be impeached, as well. But that makes it all the more imperative to start enforcing this part of the Constitution more effectively. Moreover, the Iran War is much larger and has had far worse effects than any other illegal wars of the last several decades. Obama's illegal wars were much smaller, by comparison.

I will not say much about McCarthy's analysis of the other charges, except briefly on that focused on the removal of Venezuelan dictator Nicolas Maduro. McCarthy laments that Trump left the brutal socialist regime in power (minus Maduro), but suggests that the intervention might have been legally justified by the precedent of the 1989 US military action that removed and captured Panamanian strongman Manuel Noriega. I differ for reasons I outlined here:

Defenders of the legality of Trump's actions cite the 1989 invasion of Panama, which was undertaken in large part for the purpose of apprehending Panamian dictator Manuel Noriega; like Maduro, Noriega was charged with smuggling illegal drugs into the US. But the 1989 Panama precedent does not actually justify Trump's actions. On December 15, 1989 (five days before the US invasion), Noriega foolishly announced that Panama and the US were in a "state of war," thereby creating conflict between the two countries that did not exist in the Venezuela case. In addition, Panamanian forces had killed or wounded two US military personnel in the Panama Canal area, and detained other American citizens. Unlike Noriega in 1989, the Venezuelan regime did not declare war on the US or otherwise initiate a military conflict. Thus, congressional authorization is needed to make any US military intervention constitutional.

That said, I think the Maduro intervention charge is much weaker than the Iran War and boat strike charges. The Maduro action was on a much smaller scale, and has not had anything like the same horrific effects. The abusive prosecution charge is also more compelling than that focused on Maduro. Congress would do well to focus attention on the strongest charges and convict Hegseth on them.

NEXT: S. Ct. Suspends District Court's Injunction Blocking Border Barrier Building

Ilya Somin is Visiting Professor of Law at the University of Virginia, Professor of Law at George Mason University, Simon Chair in Constitutional Studies at the Cato Institute, and author of Free to Move: Foot Voting, Migration, and Political Freedom, Democracy and Political Ignorance: Why Smaller Government is Smarter, and The Grasping Hand: Kelo v. City of New London and the Limits of Eminent Domain.

John Pistelli | From the December 2026 issue

Oshinowo Studio Completes Sierra Leone Carrier Corps Memorial in Freetown

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Commonwealth War Graves Commission (CWGC) has unveiled a new memorial in Freetown, Sierra Leone, honoring more than 900 members of the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps who died in the First and Second World Wars. Designed by Lagos-based Oshinówò Studio, the intervention was unveiled on November 7 and commemorates the Carrier Corps individually by name for the first time. The design was first revealed in May 2025.

The Commonwealth War Graves Commission (CWGC) has unveiled a new memorial in Freetown, Sierra Leone, honoring more than 900 members of the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps who died in the First and Second World Wars. Designed by Lagos-based Oshinówò Studio, the intervention was unveiled on November 7 and commemorates the Carrier Corps individually by name for the first time. The design was first revealed in May 2025.

The original Freetown Memorial, designed by Sir Edwin Lutyens, was unveiled in 1931. It serves as Sierra Leone's national memorial for those who died in the World Wars and have no known grave. Its bronze panels record the names of African soldiers from the West African Regiment, the Sierra Leone Battalion of the West African Frontier Force and the Royal Engineers Inland Water Transport. Members of the Carrier Corps, however, were not named individually and were commemorated numerically.

The original Freetown Memorial, designed by Sir Edwin Lutyens, was unveiled in 1931. It serves as Sierra Leone's national memorial for those who died in the World Wars and have no known grave. Its bronze panels record the names of African soldiers from the West African Regiment, the Sierra Leone Battalion of the West African Frontier Force and the Royal Engineers Inland Water Transport. Members of the Carrier Corps, however, were not named individually and were commemorated numerically.

The new intervention consists of a prism of structural glass permanently integrated onto the Lutyens memorial. Four structural glass fins, reaching 8.4 meters in height, are etched with the names of the fallen, and the structure incorporates 4,000 kilograms of glass. The edges of the glass are lit, and a laser beacon will emit a pillar of light into the night sky on significant days of commemoration in Sierra Leone, visible within a five-kilometer radius across Freetown.

The new intervention consists of a prism of structural glass permanently integrated onto the Lutyens memorial. Four structural glass fins, reaching 8.4 meters in height, are etched with the names of the fallen, and the structure incorporates 4,000 kilograms of glass. The edges of the glass are lit, and a laser beacon will emit a pillar of light into the night sky on significant days of commemoration in Sierra Leone, visible within a five-kilometer radius across Freetown.

Related Article Structures of Memory: 5 Unbuilt Projects on Heritage from the ArchDaily Community

The project was developed with the CWGC over 16 months by engineers, glass designers, laser consultants and construction partners from Nigeria, the Netherlands, Spain, Sierra Leone and the UK. CWGC stonemasons and bronze specialists also restored the original base, uplifting the bronze panels and renewing the stonework. The names are accompanied by an epitaph in Krio and English by poets Nii Ayikwei Parkes and Julianknxx, which reads: "from today you will be carried, you are borne, now and ever."

The project was developed with the CWGC over 16 months by engineers, glass designers, laser consultants and construction partners from Nigeria, the Netherlands, Spain, Sierra Leone and the UK. CWGC stonemasons and bronze specialists also restored the original base, uplifting the bronze panels and renewing the stonework. The names are accompanied by an epitaph in Krio and English by poets Nii Ayikwei Parkes and Julianknxx, which reads: "from today you will be carried, you are borne, now and ever."

Oshinówò Studio also designed four marker memorials in provinces from which carriers were raised. These satellite sites follow the language of the Freetown Memorial and result from a co-design process that determines the places, people and events to be memorialized, a new approach for the CWGC. The project is accompanied by a register of names, museum partnerships and educational programs for schools.

These satellite sites follow the language of the Freetown Memorial and result from a co-design process that determines the places, people and events to be memorialized, a new approach for the CWGC. The project is accompanied by a register of names, museum partnerships and educational programs for schools.

The Freetown Memorial is conceived through a material symbolism that allows it to resonate across cultures and contexts. The structure we designed is, in effect, an extension of Sir Edwin Lutyens' existing monument. By following its tapering geometry, the new monument rises higher, elevating the immense volume of names etched into glass. In this gesture, the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps are symbolically carried by their comrades, reversing the relationship between those who served and those who were served. There is a profound irony in this act: it becomes a fitting expression of gratitude to men whose sacrifice was fundamental to shaping the world we inhabit today. — Tosin Oshinówò

Q: “My Spouse Complains About My ADHD Constantly!”

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

“Start with an honest conversation that acknowledges the effects of your ADHD on your partner and explains how it thwarts your efforts to be the best possible spouse .”

Q: “How can I help my husband understand my ADHD so he’s not just complaining all the time about how hard this is for him?”

First, begin by acknowledging the truth of what he is saying: Your ADHD does, indeed, have tangible effects on him. It also has emotional effects; he likely burns much more mental energy than you can see worrying about whether you completed agreed-upon tasks.

Let down your guard for an honest conversation about the impact of your actions (or inaction) on him. Show him that he matters to you and that you empathize with his experience. You may need to spend significant time here; don’t move on until you’re positive that he really feels that you get it.

[Get This Free Download: Manage ADHD’s Impact on Your Relationship]

This sets the stage for the second part of the conversation — how ADHD thwarts your efforts to be the best possible partner. In other words, you have good intentions, even if they aren’t obvious, and you can feel equally frustrated and disappointed by what makes him upset. Also, your ADHD makes your life harder, not just his. While his complaints might be accurate, his too-frequent criticisms are not helping.

Finally, discuss how you can work together to address your hotspots. Ask: What can each of us do, or at least try to do, differently to smooth out some of our pain points? For example, do you want reminders? If so, how and when? Also, does he want to give those reminders?

It’s also helpful to discuss protocols when things don’t go according to plan. How and when can he bring up his concerns in a way that you can hear without getting defensive? How can you tell him that his proposed solution is not working for you?

Encourage your partner to focus on their experience rather than dissecting your actions and making declarations about why you did something.

For example, it’s more effective to say, “It stresses me out when you don’t follow through on commitments because then I feel like I have to take over, and I really don’t want to keep doing that,” versus saying, “You don’t worry about doing things because you know I’ll just do it if you don’t.”

[Download: 5 Ways to Improve Emotional Control at Home]

I often recommend focusing your conversations on what you want in the future, not what happened in the past.

PubMatic Is All In On Agentic Buys; How Much Power Is Too Much Power?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

PubMatic saw sizable growth in Q3, with its revenue totaling $79.6 million – up 17% from the same period last year.

Its not-so-secret sauce? Advancing AI solutions and increased demand for CTV and mobile app advertising.

CTV spend represented 21% of PubMatic’s total revenue this quarter, up 30% year over year, CFO Steve Pantelick told investors on Monday afternoon’s earnings call. Mobile app revenue grew even faster, nearly 40% year over year, and made up roughly 27% of quarterly revenue.

But it’s not just ad spend that’s evolving; it’s how the ads are bought and launched. Since PubMatic launched AgenticOS, its operating system for agentic advertising, in January, buyers have used it to run over 120 fully agentic campaigns, roughly one-third of which happened in Q3.

And the company only expects that number to go up.

When asked by an investor what parts of a campaign are still done by humans and which steps can’t be replicated by AI, PubMatic CEO Rajeev Goel suggested that whatever isn’t yet being done by AI soon will be.

Some clients are “all in on AI,” while others want to manually review each step of the campaign, Goel said, describing it as different “parts of the maturity spectrum.”

Meaning: If you don’t want AI doing your work for you, tough luck. Just “mature” a few more years.

“There’s a lot of risk in automating choice,” Jonathan Roberts, head of innovation at People Inc., cautioned listeners during the IAB roundtable discussion “Advertising in the Agentic Era” on Monday.

Although allowing an AI assistant to make purchase decisions may be a tempting convenience, it will devolve into a “winner takes all” situation for brands, which is, simply put, “bad for democracy,” said Roberts.

Not to mention the fact that what’s best for one customer isn’t best for everyone. Roberts likens AI answer engines to a bar where every customer is handed an espresso martini because that’s the average, or most common, order.

Personal agents, though, ingest a large amount of information about the consumer. “People will literally tell [them] everything, even if they shouldn’t,” Roberts quipped. That becomes an issue in terms of privacy, but also in terms of overreliance on memory. Which is to say, just because you ordered something once doesn’t mean that’s the product you want every time, or that you need another, identical product.

The sweet spot for recommendations is somewhere in between the two extremes: AI agents that prioritize real-time data and protect consumer privacy while also providing personalized responses that cater to user preferences.

Otherwise, said Roberts, “we have this kind of ‘espresso martini for everyone’ world, and that sucks.”

WPP-owned ad agency Ogilvy is partnering with Creative Artists Agency (CAA) to use the likenesses of CAA’s celebrity clients in AI-generated ads, reports Digiday.

The resulting ads will use data from CAA’s library of digital face and body scans, which it already maintains for use in deaging and postproduction work. They will also be watermarked as AI and approved by the talent featured therein.

Technically, this agreement is a kosher one as far as Hollywood is concerned. SAG-AFTRA’s AI regulations stipulate that performers are required to give consent to the use of digital replicas and must be compensated at the same rates as they would be for in-person filming.

But even though actors are on board (although only Terry Crews has publicly opted in so far), it might still be difficult for consumers to come around to the idea of AI-generated endorsements.

Sure, AI-generated content is now prevalent enough that some audience members are even voluntarily watching it. (Remember how many views “Fruit Love Island” got?) But the existing association that many users probably still have between deepfaked celebrity endorsements and online scam ads might be tougher to overcome.

Amid a difficult transitional year, The Trade Desk posts a rare quarterly revenue decline

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The latest batch of ad tech earnings disclosures was made public after the bell yesterday, with The Trade Desk posting a rare annual revenue decrease, only the second in its decade as a publicly listed company, and a sign of the scale of its turnaround challenges.

The industry’s largest independent demand-side platform, whose immediate rivals are Amazon and Google, recently trumpeted the return of The Trade Desk cofounder and CTO Dave Pickles to help mount its comeback after a challenging 18-to-24 months.

The DSP posted Q3 revenue of $736 million, representing a 0.5% annual dip – the last such dip occurred at the depths of business uncertainty during the Covid-19 pandemic – a sign of the scale of the turnaround challenge it faces.

Meanwhile, its ad tech cohorts posted revenue increases during the reporting period on the same day (see chart below), another sign of the sector’s mixed fortunes.

Jeff Green, cofounder and CEO, The Trade Desk, noted the company’s reorganization efforts of the last year, which included a 15% headcount cut during Q3, plus its third-party audience and agentic offerings, plus its plans for more transparent measurement for advertisers. “I’m extremely excited to have him [Pickles] more deeply engaged with the team as we move into this next chapter,” Green told analysts, “we spent the last year changing our company because the industry has changed and is continuing to change.”

Execs there pointed out that revenue performance during the period was “meaningfully ahead of the outlook we provided,” noting how video, including CTV, now accounts for more than half of its revenue mix, with the DSP also forecasting that its Q4 revenue would be “at least $755 million.”

In a prepared statement, Jeff Green said, “We are moving with greater urgency across the company,” but acknowledged, “we are still operating below the level of growth we expect from ourselves.”

Facing questions from analysts, Green later outlined the company’s planned tactical changes, as well as when it expects to return to more sustainable growth, noting how the company needs to “make the power of The Trade Desk easier to use, easier to adopt and easier to prove incremental outcomes.”

He added, “Open measurement is expected to better prove incremental outcomes… while open measurement will be implemented this quarter, we expect the product to improve our growth next year and provide a tailwind for years to come.”

Under further questioning from analysts, Green noted how The Trade Desk – a company that has been notably reticent over mergers and acquisitions – was more open than ever to M&A given the current technological landscape.

However, that shouldn’t be mistaken for a change in the company’s attitude to writing checks for more ad tech, even if it does lead to more lines of business. “Our philosophy on M&A has not fundamentally changed,” he told analysts.

“We’re looking [at M&A prospects] more now than we historically have, but that said, it’s also critical to take away that our standards are also higher than ever,” Green said, adding that his leadership would never want to “derail” its current trajectory “simply because we bought a company.”

Green’s comments came the same afternoon as Zeta Global posted revenue of $493 million, up 46% year on year, making it one of the fastest-growing companies in terms of revenue throughout the period, with the company further raising its Q4 revenue guidance to the range of $521 million to $525 million, a figure that would represent up to 33% annual growth.

Meanwhile, rival DSP Viant posted $110.4 million in Q3 revenue, a 29% annual increase, as well as the purchase of generative AI company Waymark, with management noting they expected Q4 revenue in the region of $130.5 million to $133.5 million.

Elsewhere, supply-side platform PubMatic posted revenue of $79.6 million, up 17% year on year, with company leadership keen to underline an ongoing industry trend that many feel is helping to bring more equity to the historic power imbalance between the buy- and sell-side of the industry.

“AI is accelerating the shift from sell-side targeting to sell-side decisioning, enabling buyers to act on richer intelligence closer to the inventory and first party [sic] insights,” noted Rajeev Goel, PubMatic CEO, in a prepared statement, which also forecast Q4 revenue in the range of $91 to $93 million, a prospective increase of 15%.

Ad Tech Briefing: Amazon’s auction trial puts agentic advertising’s trust problem in focus

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Amazon, along with its Big Tech cohorts, wants advertisers to trust its AI more, but fresh allegations from federal regulators underline concerns over such platforms.

Last week, the Federal Trade Commission published newly unredacted details from its lawsuit accusing Amazon of secretly inflating advertising prices, with similar charges against Google still fresh in the public mindset.

According to the FTC, Amazon imposed surcharges of up to 50% on ordinary shopping days and 250% during major retail events, generating an additional $87 million during the 2022 holiday season. Amazon disputes the allegations, and the legal proceedings are ongoing.

Amazon recently reported quarterly advertising revenue approaching $20 billion, with  Amazon Ads Agent – an AI-powered platform that’s likely to attract accusations of “black box” from detractors – a key part of its push to automate campaign planning, buying and optimization.

And with Amazon, the third pillar of adland’s triopoly, the key question for advertisers is whether they should hand over more control to a platform when they cannot independently verify how its existing auctions determine prices.

The FTC and 22 state attorneys general accuse Amazon of misleading advertisers about its Sponsored Products auctions.

Amazon presented these as generalized second-price auctions, where advertisers generally pay prices determined by competing bids rather than their maximum bid.

However, regulators allege Amazon introduced undisclosed reserve-price mechanisms that increased charges without corresponding competition, effectively pushing prices closer to first-price auctions.

According to the government body’s allegations, during the 2021 holiday season, Amazon increased average cost per click by 20%, with some advertisers seeing increases exceeding 200%. The complaint also alleges executives considered using reserve prices to address a $40 million revenue shortfall.

The allegations extend to how Amazon explained rising prices, i.e., whether Amazon misrepresented how its auctions worked and what advertisers were charged.

However, Amazon maintains that reserve prices reflect the value of its advertising placements, disputes the FTC’s interpretation, and argues that reserve pricing is not inherently unlawful.

For advertisers, the concern is straightforward: they can see what they paid, but cannot necessarily establish how that price was calculated.

The allegations come shortly after Amazon introduced new products designed to automate more advertising decisions.

At October’s unBoxed conference, Amazon unveiled Ads Agent and Full-Funnel Campaigns, allowing advertisers to set objectives, products, creative and budgets while its technology handles targeting, allocation and optimization.

Amazon says beta testing delivered up to 67% higher return on long-term ad spend and 29% lower costs per new-to-brand customer compared with manually configured campaigns. Those figures are company-reported.

The proposition is that advertisers can reduce manual campaign management and improve performance. However, greater automation also means relying more heavily on Amazon to explain how budgets are spent and why its systems make particular decisions.

While the FTC’s allegations concern Sponsored Products auctions, not Amazon’s new AI products, they still add weight to concerns raised about advertisers’ ability to inspect automated decisions, verify results, and understand fees.

Google’s ad tech antitrust case provides a precedent, although the conduct and markets differ.

A federal court found Google illegally monopolized publisher ad-serving and ad exchange markets – in a separate case, its search practices were also found to be monopolistic. The subsequent remedies stopped short of divestiture but imposed measures intended to improve competition and interoperability.

Both cases concern platforms whose control over advertising infrastructure limits customers’ ability to scrutinize how transactions work.

The difference is that Amazon is now asking advertisers to give its technology greater control over buying decisions, even as regulators challenge the transparency of its existing auction practices. These developments involve different businesses and issues, but they point to a broader accountability problem in digital advertising.

“We’re having to look much more closely at what happens after the click and whether those clicks are actually producing qualified leads and revenue.”

— Lindsay Marty, founder of Above the Bar Marketing, speaking to Digiday about advertisers reassessing their Google Search budgets.

ChatGPT’s ad offering may be on the rise, but the nature of its value is still hard to determine, according to numbers analyzed in recent studies.

Can web users trust ad tech’s server-side shift?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

“Trust” is one of those words that gets thrown around a lot in ad tech circles. But it’s going to become even more important if ad tech completes its server-side shift.

In response to Apple’s identity resolution crackdown, the online ad industry has rallied around server-side ways to sidestep browser-based controls. IAB Tech Lab’s Trusted Server is the biggest example of this. Not only does the open-source software promise publishers faster page-load speeds, it enables publishers to better control their ad tech stacks and minimize their exposure to browser-based blockades.

“When this initiative first started, it was pretty clear that it was time for publishers to start thinking about not continuing to keep everything within the browser itself,” said Rowena Lam, senior director of product, privacy and data at IAB Tech Lab, on the latest Digiday Podcast episode.

Taking ad calls out of the browser, however, has the effect of taking them out of users’ sights. A browser can see – and block – the calls a web page asks it to make. Once the calls run on a publisher’s server, though, users have to trust the publisher to a greater degree.

And this is where the trust in Trusted Server really matters.

Lam also noted that Trusted Server was designed from the start to support integration with consent management platforms. That would give websites a way to share a user’s consent status across the programmatic supply chain. But of course, that requires the user blindly trusting the site to do so.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

“Going server side I would consider just more privacy-preserving in general and lets publishers actually better respect the consumer’s privacy choices ultimately because when it’s not the browser and the publisher is not telling the browser ‘go and call this particular partner’ and the publisher themselves are controlling it, they can decide and be pretty surgical ultimately about who they would want to call, which partner and what data that partner can get.”

“There is a fear of just broadly server-side because it’s not automatically observable to the consumer’s browser and you can’t automatically inspect it from that point. But what we’ve done for Trusted Server in particular is we’ve made sure all of this is considered. What’s included in Trusted Server is pretty robust logging and auditing. Anyone who is looking to deploy Trusted Server gets that out of the box.”

“There’s no defaults per se in trusted server. We’re not trying to make legal determinations on behalf of publishers. What we do require publishers to do is make decisions on what exactly and how exactly they want to operate as it relates to respecting consumer privacy. That is the piece that’s built in natively to trusted server. So it’s not a matter of you’re using trusted server and it’s a free-for-all.”

Client-side vs. server-side privacy considerations

“I don’t think this conversation of consumer trust is necessarily different when we are talking about going server-side. We’re in a world today also now that there are privacy laws, regulation globally that actually mandate that publishers respect consumers’ choices about how their data is used. You have to continue to do that and do that correctly. When we’re talking client-side, there’s a lot more oopsies that can happen, whereas server-side – if you configure it incorrectly, there could be an issue – but there’s at least still a little bit more control.”

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

Reddit dangles seasonal ad credits but advertisers still want better proof it works all year round

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Reddit is offering advertisers extra incentives to spend in Q4 — the stretch of the year when budgets are biggest and competition for them is fiercest.

The social news website is offering new advertisers credits on new campaigns that go live before November 20: in the U.K., they get £500 for spending £1,000, or £2,000 for spending £2,000, according to an ad exec who recently spoke with their Reddit rep and shared an email confirming the incentives and deadline. In the U.S., the platform is also offering new advertisers $1,500 in ad credits when they spend $1,500 on the platform, per ads seen by Digiday.

After reviewing the incentives that Digiday had seen, Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha said that incentives like this “do work to reignite dormant clients’ interest to retry the platform.” However, following the decline in Google Search referrals, the general sentiment among his team’s clients is that Reddit isn’t as attractive without ChatGPT and Google sending it all that traffic.

The dip Divecha is referring to is the one Reddit disclosed on its earnings call last month when CEO Steve Huffman said October search referrals were down by more than one million a week on average, in part because of Google’s AI search features. To be clear, the credits aren’t tied to the slump. Q4 incentives are a fixture of the platform ad calendar and Reddit’s deadline for them is timed to get new campaigns running before Black Friday. That said, the extra spend could still help offset some of the hit from search, at least for a quarter.

Because incentives only go so far. Keeping advertisers around once the credits run out takes an ads platform that does what marketers need it to do, from measurement to targeting to self-serve tools. Reddit is still building toward all of that.

“Reddit is making big strides to compete with their competitor set within social with new capabilities, features across targeting, bidding and measurement but ultimately still comes in last place,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “Advertisers do like to test the waters with it but it doesn’t necessarily translate in always-on budgets.”

The incentives come at the end of a year in which the rate of Reddit’s ad revenue growth has slowed down over three consecutive quarters. In Q1 2026, Reddit recorded $625 million in ad revenue, up 74% year over year, per the company’s earnings reports. By Q2, ad revenue had increased to $762 million, the rate of growth had slipped to 64% on the same period a year ago. By Q3 2026, the platform announced $847 million in ad revenue, with growth falling again to 54% versus the same period a year ago, per its latest earnings.

“The slowdown in ad revenue growth and referral traffic may be occurring at the same time, but that doesn’t necessarily mean one is driving the other,” said Jessica Dervyn, senior principal analyst in Gartner’s Marketing Practice.

Reddit, however, points to its continued revenue growth and advertiser demand as evidence that the platform remains an attractive proposition for marketers.

“The numbers speak for themselves: we’ve grown ad revenue by more than 50% year over year for nine straight quarters, well ahead of most peers over the same timeframe,” said Natalie Maguire, director of global B2B communications at Reddit in an emailed statement to Digiday. “We continue to see strong demand from advertisers as they increasingly turn to Reddit to drive results across the funnel. Our ad incentive program has stayed consistent over the last year and we have not increased our incentives amount. It’s an ongoing part of our business and a standard practice across the industry.”

It’s a thought not lost on some advertisers.

Take James Mortimer at Roast, for example. He said that his team finds the platform “really valuable” for advertisers because the users are so engaged.

“There’s quite a lot of people on there [Reddit] you can’t reach on Meta,” he said. Mortimer acknowledged that Reddit isn’t for every advertiser, but said it was “definitely valuable for the right advertisers that can work on there.”

However, audience engagement alone isn’t necessarily enough to convince advertisers to shift more of their budgets to Reddit. Measurement remains an important part of the equation.

“That’s something where Meta is really good now, so it’s harder to make the case for a client who is spending on Meta already to then take budget and put it into another channel [like Reddit], which might not be as effective,” said Mortimer.

New Gen’s strategy director Mark Bellamy had a similar take, saying that search referrals are part of the story but not the main thing holding back ad spend on Reddit. The bigger picture, he said, is the ad product.

“Reddit is still a test-and-learn platform for us, not somewhere we’d confidently put full-funnel budget,” he said. “You can get good CPMs and decent awareness. What we haven’t seen enough of is proof that you can build an ecosystem there that drives quality traffic and nurtures it. Until we do, we’ll be cautious about recommending significant media spend.”

That puts the onus on Reddit to demonstrate that advertisers can get meaningful returns from the platform beyond the initial incentive. While promotional offers can bring new advertisers through the door, converting those trials into long-term investment is a different challenge entirely.

The case for and against AI token capping

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

When one of the world’s most valuable companies starts to get serious about AI waste, it’s time to pay attention. Apple is placing a hard cap of $200 on its engineers’ daily AI token use, and may reduce non-engineers’ token allowance to $25, Bloomberg reported last week (Nov. 4).

A handful of ad agencies have already embraced token capping this year — using AI software, or a bespoke tool, to prevent users from consuming more than a set amount of AI compute as the costs of AI transformation become clearer. Apple’s policy change indicates that more companies could follow that path, but there are tradeoffs involved in this strict approach to costs.

Agencies considering capping token usage must weigh up the risk they could douse their employees’ creativity by limiting their access to the tech.

AI costs — from tokens to licensing and subscriptions — are likely as cheap as they’ll ever be. While manageable today, they’re kept low because of the enormous capital expenditures of companies like Meta, OpenAI and Anthropic. Eventually, those firms will want a return on those investments, meaning today’s prices might not be stable for long.

With that in mind, marketing agencies are building the infrastructure of AI accountability today, rather than wait for a crisis. Performance agency Roast, for example, has capped the amount of AI tokens its staffers can use since March.

“It’s up to the individual to use those tokens responsibly, and I think the benefit of that is then it’s making people think about what use cases they use tokens for,” John Campbell, head of innovation and AI at Roast, told Digiday.

The company allots a set amount of tokens to each employee within its Claude subscription, Campbell said. Higher individual caps can be provided at particularly busy times. He noted that the limit had stopped some users from relying on the tech for repetitive tasks that might be better delegated to non-AI software tools.

Video and image production generally represents the most token-intensive activity for creative agencies; media agencies experimenting with agentic tools for planning and buying must also grapple with runaway operating costs.

U.S. media agency PMG places a $50 daily limit on staff AI usage through its Alli4U portal. Staff that hit the cap more than once can be assigned a larger token budget, or receive advice on using different AI models, Dillon Larberg, consulting and strategy director, told Digiday in September. “That provides us a human in the loop moment to meet with teams or meet with individuals,” he said.

While agencies might still want to allow staff the flexibility to experiment and play with AI tools they can use tiered capping systems to provide teams with enough leeway to pilot something new, without burning up the budget.

“If you say you have a limit of $30, $25, $10 a day, you’ll find people become very creative in how they get this stuff done based on that constraint,” said Jonathan Whiteside, global executive vp at Dept, which caps team AI usage rather than individual costs.

“Constraints are sometimes useful for us to find new ways of working,” Whiteside added. “People start to think much more carefully about, ‘Well, do I actually need to use AI to do this?’”

AI costs, including the price of tokens, won’t stay low forever, so agencies should make hay while the sun’s shining. Intentionally cutting back before compute costs rise would be like abstaining from MoviePass or WeWork in the glorious days of zero interest rates. Agencies can chew through tokens while they develop the best uses for AI tech now, while it’s cheap to do so.

Some agencies use soft AI caps, rather than a hard limit. “When someone maxes out their spend for that tier, they show their work and their output to their department head,” explained Daniel Gilbert, CEO of Brainlabs, in September. He described the company’s approach as one that embraced “checkpoints” rather than caps. “The department head goes through the work with them, and if it’s good work, then they push them into the next tier.”

Such an approach preserves staff autonomy while keeping a lid on AI spending for agencies that want to to let a hundred flowers, or AI agents, bloom.

There are also means of nudging staff into cost-conscious AI decisions, without placing concrete limits on their usage. Gateway systems, or “gating,” refers to a tool which reroutes a user from a token-intensive AI model to a less powerful (and cheaper) model, if it’s better suited to a user’s project. If they’re using ChatGPT to draft their LinkedIn posts, they probably don’t need Astra, its latest and most powerful model.

Whiteside said that model selection is “a big factor” in keeping costs down, but that gating systems or staff training can provide a more appropriate guardrail than an individual token allowance.

As the real costs of AI-enabled marketing no services become clearer, expect more agencies and brands to consider whether hard caps or flexibility are most important.

Toxic secrets are hiding in Arctic ice

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

A century-old pollution trail leads scientists back to Montana mining

At the turn of the twentieth century, the Anaconda Smelter in Montana spewed arsenic into the air — those heavy metal particles that are now trapped in Arctic ice cores.

Ice cores record Earth’s secrets, even the dirty ones.

Some of the cylindrical sections of ice extracted from the Arctic contain elevated levels of arsenic, lead and other heavy metals that circulated in our planet’s atmosphere in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. Those pollutants, which have been tied to declines in IQ and higher rates of certain cancers, were probably emitted by a mining and smelting company in Montana whose grounds are now designated as multiple Superfund sites, the researchers report November 3 in Nature Sustainability.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

The ice cores contain tiny particles that were once airborne — including pollutants, volcanic ash and salt from ocean spray — and fell to our planet’s surface within a few days or weeks. In polar regions, that fallout combines with snow and is preserved as ice.

Joseph McConnell, an environmental scientist at the Desert Research Institute, and his collaborators recently analyzed 10 ice cores collected across Canada, Greenland and Russia. The cores contained material that had fallen to Earth as early as 210 BCE, but the interval spanning the Industrial Revolution in the United States — from roughly 1870 to 1914 — was what caught the team’s collective eye.

In the ice layers from that period, arsenic concentrations in some of the ice cores were up to 15 times higher than pre-Industrial Revolution levels. Because the heavy metal exposure has been tied to upticks in the rates of certain cancers, McConnell and his team set out to determine the most likely source of all that pollution.

The largest increases in arsenic existed in the ice cores from Greenland. That finding was a big hint, McConnell says, because atmospheric circulation patterns tend to move material from west to east. “The only way you can explain that pattern of deposition is if the emissions are coming from North America,” McConnell says. “Had it been from Europe, we would have seen much larger increases in the Russian Arctic.”

The most likely source, the team concluded, was a massive copper mining and smelting operation in southwestern Montana operated by the Anaconda Copper Mining Company. It was one of the largest in North America during the Industrial Revolution and, unlike other copper mining operations in the area, it worked with copper ore that contained heavy metals like arsenic and lead. It was no secret that those toxic metals were entering the atmosphere, says Fred Quivik, a historian and the author of Smoke and Tailings, a book about the environmental history of copper smelting in Montana. “Ranchers and farmers started seeing their livestock die from arsenic poisoning.”

Based on the concentrations of arsenic in the ice cores and models of how material disperses in the atmosphere, McConnell and his colleagues estimated that the Anaconda Copper Mining Company was emitting roughly 30 tons of arsenic per day into the atmosphere in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. By comparison, Chile, currently the world’s largest producer of copper and home to more than 20 mining operations, collectively emits roughly one quarter as much arsenic today.

Beginning in 1919, the Anaconda Copper Mining Company installed devices near its smokestacks and other facilities to capture arsenic and prevent its release into the atmosphere. Arsenic emissions dropped by about 60 percent as a result, the ice core data showed, but by then hundreds of thousands of nearby residents had already been exposed for decades. That exposure could have been lessened, McConnell says, if the Anaconda Copper Mining Company hadn’t waited so long to install the pollution-abatement technology. “They delayed installation for about a decade.”

These new findings should be combined with census records to trace the effects of this pollution on human health in the 19th and 20th centuries, McConnell and his team suggest. “We hope that epidemiologists will take this information and really investigate potential impacts on human populations.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

J.R. McConnell et al. Widespread North American pollution and health impacts from early 20th C copper production. Nature Sustainability. 2026. doi: 10.1038/s41893-026-01975-9

Australian Plugin Vehicle Market Steady in October — Update

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

… with a spotlight on the Great Wall Motor ORA 5 rising star.

CleanTechnica readers were probably hoping for the surge to keep surging Down Under. I was. However, it appears to have remained steady. Not the flash in the pan petrolheads were hoping for at least. In September 2026, 108,778 passenger vehicles were sold, including all drivetrains. See the charts here.

Battery electric vehicles (BEVs) achieved a market share of 24.2% (26,287 units), outselling petrol-only and diesel-only vehicles. Add in 10,072 plugin hybrid (PHEV) sales and you get a plugin penetration rate of approximately 34%. PHEV sales are the fastest growing segment in Australia — increasing 122% year on year. For those who care, 17,843 HEVs sold in October (a YoY increase of 20%). I am looking forward to peak HEV, followed by peak PHEV, as we have seen in more mature Scandinavian markets.

Australia’s EV penetration rate (including BEV and PHEV) has hovered in the low to mid 30% for the last few months. Not sure what the future holds, but even just maintaining this rate for a few months will make a difference. Nothing beats lived experience. My neighbours are now asking about how much it costs to fill up. One came for coffee yesterday and stayed to go for a drive in our new Model Y. He was very impressed with the tech, the finish, and above all the running costs. He confided that he had just filled up his wife’s Honda Civic — AU$82 — and his Isuzu D Max — AU$121.

On a recent shopping expedition, we counted 10 electric cars in the car park, out of 50 vehicles. Not just Tesla and BYD, but also Zeekr, Geely, the GWM ORA 5, and a Deepal. Battery electric vehicles are making their presence felt. But not all my friends can see them. They can spot Tesla and BYD vehicles, but they tell me they don’t see any other EVs on the road. Yes, we have tried to educate them, but it is not working just yet. “See if it has an exhaust pipe. Does it have a sealed front?”

One car that I am seeing in ever increasing numbers is the refreshed Great Wall Motors ORA — now in an SUV body and a much cheaper price. When it launched back in 2023, I expected great things (see our review here). Sadly, the Funky Cat (as it is known in China) did not live up to expectations. It was the cheapest EV in Australia at the time (AU$46,000) but couldn’t compete with the BYD Dolphin and the MG 4. Now, relaunched as an SUV and with a more appropriate price (AU$34,000 — scratch that, just got an email that knocks another grand off the price), I am seeing them everywhere — 6 on the highway just yesterday.

So, on to the best-selling BEVs on the Australian market in October 2026:

Pretty much the same as last month. Obviously, BYD was the best-selling brand, closely followed by Tesla. BYD has six cars among the top twenty BEVs. There are no cars from Korean, Japanese, or European carmakers in the top ten BEV numbers. When all powertrains are considered, BYD is the second highest selling brand behind Toyota in the Australian market. There seems to be some talk in the auto news about Tesla and BYD being considered “legacy” brands — who would have thought?

Expect big things from Geely as it aggressively expands throughout Australia. Geely recently announced that it has opened 100 dealerships over the past 18 months. Its EX5 sales figures are putting pressure on the competition in the medium-SUV-segment Mazda CX-5, Hyundai Tucson, Mitsubishi Outlander, and Nissan X-Trail.

Utes still dominate the Australian market. When a compelling BEV offering becomes available, the top ten may change again. I was excited to see the KGM Musso BEV ute, but it has failed to gain traction. Only 269 have sold in the past few months. The Toyota BEV Hilux only has 14 units in the country — likely to be demonstrators. PHEV utes seem to be doing much better, led by the BYD Shark. Over 15,000 have sold so far this year.

Now for some newsy titbits: Dealers for established brands are calling for a temporary ban on new car brands (read Chinese made cars) entering Australia, as they cannot compete. The boss of the Australian Automotive Dealer Association says the industry has reached a crisis point, with the last quarter delivering the first loss since the global financial crisis. Chinese brands now own 36% of new car sales.

“This is a clear warning that the commercial settings underpinning our industry are becoming unsustainable,” Mr Voortman said. He speculates on the possible risks to consumers: “… the influx of new brands also puts consumers at risk, as it can lead to rapid depreciation of vehicles, a lag in parts supply, longer repair times, and orphaned vehicles should those companies choose to leave the country.”

Though, what we are seeing in Australia is that legacy brands, mainly European, are the ones leaving the country.

The FUD continues as misconceptions and outdated information are rolled out as facts. If you want to revisit these arguments, check this out. Trigger warning: some of you may wish to yell at the screen. The author twists himself in knots trying to make the argument that the wrong Australians are buying electric vehicles. I would contend that the right people are buying EVs — those commuting from the outer suburbs of our sprawling cities. Data shows that it is these people who are behind the EV sales boom we are currently experiencing, and these are the ones benefitting most from not paying inflated petrol costs caused by Iran’s unwillingness to admit defeat and grovel at the feet of the orange one. Please excuse my sarcasm. All of this is documented in the 10th annual Electric Vehicle Council Report. We reviewed this report here.

Despite the desperate attempt to promulgate fear of “these here new-fangled doodads” — the creation of doubt and uncertainly — every month shows how intelligent the average consumer is as they seek a better, electrified, and brighter future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

More than 300 Solar & Storage Companies Urge Congress to Pass Bipartisan Permitting Reform to Accelerate Clean Energy Deployment, Lower Costs

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

WASHINGTON D.C. — More than 300 solar and storage companies are urging Congress to pass the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), S. 5653, warning that outdated federal permitting processes and grid infrastructure bottlenecks are delaying critical energy projects and driving up electricity costs.

In a letter to congressional leaders, the companies call on lawmakers to quickly send the bipartisan legislation to the President’s desk. The signatories note that solar and storage account for 70% of new electricity capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026, while $120 billion in clean energy investment remains caught in permitting delays.

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA).

BAAJA would modernize the federal permitting system by ensuring fair treatment for all technologies, protecting lawfully issued permits from politically motivated delays, establishing predictable timelines for federal reviews and judicial proceedings, modernizing transmission planning, and expanding distributed energy resources’ access to wholesale electricity markets.

New SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.

The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern. Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.

About SEIA®: The Solar Energy Industries Association® (SEIA) is leading the transformation to a clean energy economy. SEIA works with its 1,200 member companies and other strategic partners to fight for policies that create jobs in every community and shape fair market rules that promote competition and the growth of reliable, low-cost solar power. Founded in 1974, SEIA is the national trade association for the solar and solar + storage industries, building a comprehensive vision for the Solar+ Decade through research, education and advocacy. Visit SEIA online at www.seia.org and follow @SEIA on Twitter, LinkedIn, and Instagram.

Love Is Blind's Morrey Claps Back Over Season 11 Reunion Clash With John

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Morrey Eglin is clapping back at the controversy over his explosive argument with Love Is Blind costar John Shannon at the season 11 reunion regarding the latter’s alleged emotional infidelity.

“I actually picked up a new book today and I’m having a little bit of trouble, if you can help me,” Morrey, 32, quipped via a Monday, November 9, Instagram Reel before shifting the camera to reveal the book he referenced was the Holy Bible.

He then declared, “I just can’t seem to find the chapter where it says you can cheat on your wife. If anyone knows what chapter that is and they want to let me know? I’d really appreciate it. I just really want to get on the same page as you guys. No pun intended.”

“Other than that, I’m going to keep reading,” he teased.

During the Sunday, November 8, reunion, Morrey got into a heated shouting match after romantic rival John, 33, confessed that he’d committed emotional infidelity by exchanging text messages with a stripper during his brief marriage to Sarah Williams. (It was reported prior to the Love Is Blind season 11 reunion airing that Sarah, 34, filed for divorce from John in June after separating earlier in the year.)

Chaos erupted at the reunion taping when John’s father insisted that, while his son had done “a stupid thing,” he had not actually “cheated” on Sarah.

“There’s nothing to defend,” John told his dad, adding that he took “full accountability” for his transgression.

Season 11 cast member Scott Shuman jumped in to back up John, calling him “a good man” and insisting that his costar had “prayed over every single person” at a cast breakfast.

“Nobody brings up the fact that [Sarah] goes out three times a week,” Scott, 38, alleged.

John begged Scott to stop defending him before Morrey interrupted Scott in to demand, “Hey, why don’t you shut the f*** up?!”

“What does the Bible think about f***ing cheating?” Morrey yelled at Scott.

There was tension between Morrey and John long before the reunion as they both dated Sarah while in the pods. Morrey ultimately quit the experiment when Sarah chose to pursue an engagement with John over him.

“I fell for Sarah, and I had no other connection as strong with her,” Morrey exclusively told Us in October. “And if it wasn’t her, it was nobody there.”

The bouncer conceded during his chat with Us that he “wasn’t too caught off guard” when Sarah opted to pursue things with John.

“She genuinely cared what I had to say. … I said things to her that I’ve never said to a girl. You know what I mean?” he shraed with Us. “Relationship-wise or anything like that, where she really just was a safe space for me. And I do genuinely think she cared about me and wants the best for me. And I want the same for her. I think she’s brilliant and has so many great qualities and made the best decision for herself. And I really just wanted her to be happy.”

Love Is Blind season 11 is available to stream worldwide via Netflix.

5 Years In, Mustang Mach-E Battery Data Shows What Real-World Electric Vehicle Durability Looks Like

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E customers began adding something important to the Ford electric vehicle battery validation work: real-world ownership at scale.

As part of our quality process, design and validation engineering starts long before a vehicle reaches a customer. We model battery performance, run physical tests, expose packs to heat, cold, and charging profiles, and build in margin for demanding real-world conditions.

These rigorous quality tests are designed to help us understand how battery systems perform across a wide range of climates, charging behaviors, and driving conditions before customers get behind the wheel.

Then David Blenkle drove past 400,000 miles on the original battery in his 2022 Mustang Mach-E. He gave us a real-world example of what is possible — and begged the question of whether he’s an outlier or not.

His battery health has remained strong through unusually high-mileage use. His experience gave our team a chance to compare one exceptional ownership case with what we are seeing across the broader fleet.

The bigger question was if David’s experience reflected a broader pattern. So, we decided to look back at the Mustang Mach-E owners who started it all: The Class of 2021.

Over five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E vehicles — model year 2021 — reached customers. Those electric SUVs have been on the road, in customers’ hands, ever since.

We analyzed the battery state-of-health data for the entire fleet.

Here is what the Class of 2021 looks like at the five-year mark:

More than 36,000 vehicles. Two billion all-electric miles, across all 50 states and across Canada.

Our Class of 2021 has almost reached Neptune — not a practical comparison, but a useful way to understand the scale and distance.

The number I look at closely is the spread. Ninety-five percent of those vehicles fall between 87% and 97% health. Averages can hide variation, so that range matters.

In some cases, David has driven 300,000 miles more than many in our 2021 cohort. Then there are also the people who are road-warriors, like David.

More than 2,000 Class of 2021 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery, averaging better than 91% battery health. A smaller group of high-mileage drivers — more than 30 drivers past 200,000 miles — is averaging 88%.1

David’s mileage remains exceptional, but the broader Class of 2021 data shows he is part of a larger pattern of strong battery health across real-world use.

Long-term battery health remains one of the biggest questions for many people considering an electric vehicle, and real-world data like this helps make that conversation more concrete.

Two billion customer-driven miles is meaningful because it reflects how people use their vehicles — commuting, road-tripping, and running their everyday lives.

And it’s not just the 2021 Mustang Mach-E.

Aggregated across the full battery-electric lineup released over the last five years in the U.S. and Canada, the pattern holds: our packs are designed to last at least 10 years, and in the wild they’re maintaining, on average, greater than 90% health at 100,000 miles.2

The Class of 2021 is the cohort old enough, right now, to tell the most detailed version of that story.

While this story focuses on the 2021 Mustang Mach-E, the learning doesn’t stop there.

Ford uses field data from vehicles on the road to inform current and future electric vehicle programs such as the Universal Electric Vehicle platform and Fathom. This includes how we think about battery management, charging guidance, and long-term ownership confidence.

Battery chemistry is complicated, but a few simple everyday habits can help support long-term battery health:

Owners should not have to become battery chemistry experts to take good care of their electric vehicles.

The 2021 Mustang Mach-E vehicles in this data set use NCM battery chemistry, where frequent full charges are generally not needed for daily use. Some newer Ford EV batteries use LFP chemistry, which benefits from an occasional charge to 100% to help the battery management system stay calibrated.

Ford over time has implemented new features, like guidance in the Ford App, to help owners look after their batteries, specific to the vehicle and battery system they drive.

I have spent my career on the battery side of this business — cell chemistry, pack design, thermal management, and the systems that help a battery perform over time.

For an owner, the battery is central to the EV experience. It shapes confidence in every commute, road trip, and charging stop.

This is one of the most valuable feedback loops we have had at this scale: more than 36,000 vehicles, five-plus years, and billions of real-world miles.

The data is encouraging, and the learnings continue to inform how Ford approaches battery systems, software guidance, and future electric vehicle programs.

The Class of 2021 has added a real-world chapter to what our validation work was designed to understand. And we have many more electric miles ahead of us.

By Kevin Vander Laan, the Technical Leader of High-Voltage Battery Cells and Systems at Ford.

1. Battery state-of-health data is as of August 2026 for all 21MY Mustang Mach-E vehicles sold to U.S. and Canada retail and fleet customers, covering approximately 36,000 owners with an average ownership period of 5.2 years. Figures reflect usable battery capacity.

2. Based on a 2026 manufacturer field data study of high-voltage battery state of health on Ford battery electric vehicles.

Experience The Gales Of November With This Real-Time Great Lakes Simulator

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The wreck of the SS Edmund Fitzgerald became a bit of a meme last year during the 50th anniversary of the disaster. Gen Zers rediscovered the great Gordon Lightfoot song and it was all over the interwebz. Those youths can now experience the thrill, terror and wonder of the Great Lakes firsthand with my new favorite obsession, the website In The Lakes; a free site which shows you the real-time conditions on any one of the great lakes without tracking your data.

Using buoys located on Lake Superior, Lake Michigan, Lake Huron, Lake Erie, and Lake Ontario and honorable mention Lake St. Claire, In The Lake users can view a digital simulation of conditions. Different colors on the map represent real-time wave heights. As of the writing of this story, both Lake Superior and Lake Michigan are mostly red, indicative of choppy waters and waves reaching 10 feet or more. My first "Float" sent me just north of Sault Ste. Marie in Lake Superior where it was, predictably, cold, grey, and extra choppy — just the sort of thing the Edmund Fitz faced on that cold day in November, 1975.

But it's not all rough seas and wind. Pick a calmer section of lake and spend a few hours of your work day sipping a beverage, listening to a radio that features local radio stations as well as multiple genre stations, or meditate. The meditate feature is fairly entertaining, with inspirational messages meant to resonant with folks from the Great Lakes. The last time I pressed it, a message popped up reading "Somewhere, someone's shoveling a driveway. Not you. Not today." Damn right.

But it's not just floaties. You can find the option to "Take the helm in these conditions." And the familiar lake scene will load with a kayak, schooner, or even a Coast Guard ice breaking ship. I tried out the kayak to see what it was like for my husband when he puttered around Lake Superior on his own kayak, a feat that nearly killed him. You can also dive the depths of the lakes, or write a message and pop it in a bottle, then set it into the lake and see where the real forecast takes it.

In the Experiences tab you can take on several adventures from repairing the Cort for sailing duties to Cruising the Soo Locks. There's also a plethora of adventures to go on. I opted for the Brigade to the Rendezvous, an 1815 canoe trip to deliver beaver furs to Fort William, a midway trading point between the Midwest and Montreal. Teachers can also find a dedicated hub with tons of resources to go over critical things related to the Great Lakes, appropriate for certain grade levels.

Or keep it simple and continue to enjoy the occasional float on one of the Great Lakes. You can use it to escape your kids as easily as educate them, after all. An extra click will allow your setting to reset (change) every five minutes without your interference. So relax, ride the waves, and beware the gales of November.

The Crunchbase Unicorn Board Tops 1,900 Companies In October, With 34 Companies Joining — Half Under 4 Years Old

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A total of 34 companies joined The Crunchbase Unicorn Board in October, adding more than $96 billion in value and $14.8 billion in total funding to the board. The board also reached a new record of 1,900 private companies last month, including an all-time high of more than 1,000 such startups based in the U.S.

Last month’s new unicorn cohort also highlighted how quickly this AI cycle is awarding some companies with billion-dollar-plus valuations: Half of October’s new unicorns are less than 4 years old, and nine companies that joined the board during the month did so via seed or Series A raises.

Leading industries for unicorn formation last month were frontier labs, agentic AI, aerospace and semiconductors. The U.S. accounts for 23 of the companies, while China and Korea each added three. Estonia added two, a record for the nation in a single month. Singapore, Germany and Japan each added one new unicorn.

The most valuable of the newly minted unicorns were electrical infrastructure company Axiom Solutions International, TypeSafe AI  a model developer of Jev, and agentic AI assistant company Manus AI.

Familiar names topped the list of investors backing the latest cohort. Andreessen Horowitz has led rounds across seven portfolio companies over time, Sequoia Capital and General Catalyst in four companies and Eclipse three companies from this batch.

Here are October’s new unicorn companies by industry segment.

The Crunchbase Unicorn Board is a curated list that includes private unicorn companies with post-money valuations of $1 billion or more and is based on Crunchbase data. New companies are added to the Unicorn Board as they reach the $1 billion valuation mark as part of a funding round.

The unicorn board does not reflect internal company valuations — such as those set via a 409a process for employee stock options — as these differ from, and are more likely to be lower than, a priced funding round. We also do not adjust valuations based on investor writedowns, which change quarterly, as different investors will not value the same company consistently within the same quarter.

Funding to unicorn companies includes all private financings to companies that are tagged as unicorns, as well as those that have since graduated to The Exited Unicorn Board.

Exits analyzed here only include the first time a company exits.

Please note that all funding values are given in U.S. dollars unless otherwise noted. Crunchbase converts foreign currencies to U.S. dollars at the prevailing spot rate from the date funding rounds, acquisitions, IPOs and other financial events are reported. Even if those events were added to Crunchbase long after the event was announced, foreign currency transactions are converted at the historic spot price.
