The Plant Doctor Is In: How to Save a Dying Houseplant

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

You saw a plant you loved in a shop or nursery. You brought it home, and for months it warmed your heart. But then it started to decline. What are you doing wrong? Is it dying? Can you save it?

For answers, we talked with self-described “Plant Doctor” Christopher Satch, who has a master’s degree in botany from Rutgers University, teaches plant science at the New York Botanic Garden, and is involved with the Manhattan Orchid Society and the American Orchid Society. As Plant Doctor, Chris fields calls and email from people seeking advice about ailing houseplants.

The best way to keep a plant happy, he says, is to learn what it wants before you buy it—and only buy plants that suit the conditions you can offer. If you live in a ground-floor apartment with little light, say, don’t get a plant that will only thrive with tons of direct sunshine.

How can you diagnose a dying plant’s problem in time to cure it? “A lot of symptoms overlap with many causes,” says Chris. “You have to piece together the puzzle to diagnose the problem.” Here’s how to cure your plant’s ailment.

Symptoms: “If the leaves are turning yellow, the soil is moist, and you can see fungus growing at the base, you’re overwatering,” says Chris. Customers are sternly warned about how often their new plants will need water.

Solution: “Keep cacti and succulents in the sun, which cooks them dry very fast—that’s what they like,” Chris says. “Give these plants a dry rest, maybe a week, and then water them. Shade plants like ferns want their soil to be moist for a little while. Let them approach dryness—but then hit them with water right away.”

Prevention: No plant likes its roots sitting in water, which is why the pot needs good drainage—either a hole in the bottom or a layer of lava rocks or recycled terracotta shards (they’re porous, so they absorb excess water and slowly release it).

Symptoms: If leaves look droopy and are falling off, it’s a good sign that the plant’s not getting enough water.

Solution: “The goal is to saturate the soil so it’s evenly moist, and then let it dry out before watering again,” says Chris. “Most indoor plants are tropical, and they like warm water, not hot or cold.”

Chris outlines two basic watering techniques. The first: “Pour a little water into the center of the pot, let it sink in, pour a little more, and keep doing that until the soil is saturated.” How much water in all? A good rule of thumb is about a quarter to a third of the pot’s volume.

The second technique: soaking. “Put the potted plant in a sink or bowl and pour water slowly onto the top of the soil. Keep going until about a half inch of water has gone through the pot and collected in the sink or bowl. Then let the plant soak—maybe even a full day. Take it out and let it drip dry before putting it back in its planter or plate.” (This method only works when the pot has a drainage hole in the bottom—it can even be plastic.)

Prevention: “Sometimes people just dump water on the plant and it immediately flows through and comes out the bottom,” says Chris. “Your plant has not been watered—if you stick your finger into the soil you’ll see that the center of the root ball is still bone dry. That’s why soaking is important.”

Tip: That trick about putting ice cubes in the pot so the water is released slowly? Forget it.

Symptoms: You can blast most indoor plants with light and they’ll be fine, because the sun is much weaker when it comes through a window. But plants that prefer low light, like ferns and calathea, will be burned by too much sun—blanched leaves are a sign. On the other hand, plants that are not getting enough light may get spindly and stretched out or start dropping leaves, says Chris.

Solution: Most houseplants prefer medium to bright light. “Think of the fiddle-leaf fig, which people plant as outdoor hedges in Florida. They love the sun, and they’ll drop leaves in response to lower light.”

Prevention: Artificial light may help. If the natural light in your home is really low, artificial light can make plants happier. No bulb offers the full spectrum of light that plants need—not even the ones designed as grow lights—but using a more intense bulb will increase the effect. Chris recommends an LED or CFL (compact fluorescent) bulb (screw-in, not tubes), as long as it’s 1,000 lumens or more; either warm or cool is fine.

Symptoms: Too much fertilizer and “your plant will get crispy edges, called salt burn, and the leaves may turn brown or black,” Chris says. “Basically, the plant will start falling apart very fast.” But too little fertilizer may cause a plant to stop thriving;  fertilizer is important for longevity. “When a plant grows in nature, the soil is infinite; the roots can keep stretching out to find new nutrients,” Chris says. “But in a pot, that’s their universe and you’re essentially their god. Once the nutrients have been exhausted, the plant says ‘Uh-oh, what am I going to do?’

Solution: “You have to be their savior, and add fertilizer to provide nutrients—it’s literally a multivitamin for the plant,” says Chris. Follow the instructions on the label.

Prevention: Use crystals, liquid, or slow-release pellets as recommended.

Symptoms:  Chris says customers email him asking, Why isn’t my plant growing? “I look at the photo and write back, ‘Do you not notice how big the plant is in relation to the pot?’ It’s like trying to jam your foot into a shoe that’s two sizes too small!”

Solution: A rule of thumb is that the volume of the plant should be two-thirds above ground and one-third below ground. The soil level should be within an inch or two of the rim, depending on the pot size, so when you pour in water it can pool before seeping in.

Prevention: When you buy a plant that comes in a plastic pot, repot it right away. “Plants are sold overgrown; they’re not meant to live in those pots. They need room to grow,” says Chris.

Tip: Don’t let your soil get too old. “Potting mix does decay and get mucky over time,” says Chris. Even if you’re fertilizing regularly, swap out some of the soil every year or two. It’s also a good opportunity to see if your plant needs a larger pot.

Symptoms: Dry air is very bad, causing plants to crisp their leaves, says Chris.

Solution:  Keep plants well away from vents, heaters, radiators, and air conditioners. To combat low humidity, he offers three pieces of advice: “Humidifier, humidifier, humidifier.” Get the largest one you can—it’s great for human health as well. If you only have a dinky one, put it right next to the plants.

Prevention: Misting can help if it’s done regularly, but as soon as the mist dries, the effect is gone. You can also group plants with similar needs to create a microclimate—but note that if you get pests, every plant will be infested.

Symptoms: Mealy bugs look like tiny white cotton balls. Other bugs look like, well, bugs.

Solution: “Using an insecticide like Raid is like using an atom bomb to knock down a house,” says Chris. “And those chemicals can hurt the plant.” He recommends washing the plant with warm water, then wiping it down to remove as many bugs as possible. Follow up with a spray of horticultural oil or insecticidal soap, being careful to reach the crevices and the undersides of the leaves.

Prevention: Keep your plant clean (dust or wipe its leaves gently) and check it regularly for bugs.

“Most of the time those little plastic tags you get with a plant are useless,” says Chris. Ask the person you buy it from; a good nursery should know what they’re selling. Or send your question (with a photo) to your local botanic garden.

“If it’s brown or looks dead, it probably is dead,” says Chris. But some woody plants, like the rubber tree and the fiddle-leaf fig, can recover even if they’ve died back to the stalk. “Blast it with light, give it a little fertilizer, water it when it’s dry, and it’ll say ‘Life is worth living! Let’s regrow!’ You could have the same luck with plants like pothos and monstera, as long as there’s a little vine or stem left.”

On the other hand, if a plant’s too far gone—and definitely not beautifying your home—it can be wise to cut your losses and start again with something fresh.

“Most indoor plants will do absolutely fine on a steady diet of dappled sun and water when they’re dry,” Chris says reassuringly. “Cacti and succulents need more bright light and dry soil; ferns need wetter conditions. And then there’s Sansevieria, which deserves all the respect—whether it’s under artificial light or blasted with direct sun, it’ll just keep growing.”

N.B.: This post was first published December 2018; it has been updated with new information, links, and photos.

Startup Founders Are Using AI To File Patents. Here’s Where DIY Becomes A Liability.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Patents can help startups establish competitive advantage, attract investors and protect innovations. But the traditional patent process is expensive, slow and difficult to navigate, particularly for companies with limited resources.

AI is changing that equation. General-purpose tools can research existing patents, document inventions and generate drafts that look like patent applications in minutes. Handling the process themselves can seem like an easy way for founders to save money.

But AI’s capabilities are jagged. AI can perform some tasks remarkably well while missing legal and strategic issues that determine a patent’s commercial value. An application can look sophisticated while providing little meaningful protection.

For example, a patent’s claims define its legal boundaries and what you can stop competitors from doing. Mistakes involving claim scope may remain hidden until an investor, acquirer or competitor scrutinizes the company’s intellectual property.

The question isn’t whether startups should use AI. It’s how much founders can safely do themselves and when legal expertise becomes essential.

Founders can use AI to organize technical documentation, summarize existing patents and identify potentially relevant prior art. Turning engineering notes into an organized invention disclosure, for example, can reduce preparation costs and help counsel focus on strategy.

But the opportunity extends beyond DIY. The right counsel can orchestrate AI across the workflow, coordinating invention discovery, drafting, drawings and quality checks. AI can help counsel explore alternative implementations with the inventors, identify disclosure gaps and check support for claims. Attorneys can direct that work, evaluate the results and connect each step to best protect business needs.

That combination can produce more comprehensive applications with substantially greater efficiency. It is fundamentally different from generating an application with a chatbot and asking a lawyer to review it afterward.

The cheapest filing option is not necessarily the most economical. A poorly prepared application can create false confidence while leaving valuable innovations exposed.

Counsel can help decide what to patent versus maintain as a trade secret, prioritize filings within the company’s budget and assess deadlines before disclosure or filing limits the available options. An internal process that competitors cannot observe or reverse engineer, for example, may be better kept as a trade secret.

Claim scope requires particular judgment. A fraud detection claim tied to one algorithm may leave competitors free to deliver the same functionality using another. Overly broad claims may overlap with existing technology. Valuable claims target what competitors must do to compete. Ideally, a single identifiable competitor can infringe them, and that infringement can be detected and proven.

The specification must describe alternatives and variations that support broader claims later without unnecessarily limiting their scope. AI can assist, but strategic judgment must guide the work.

Filing volume says little about commercial value. Investors and acquirers will look beyond the number of pending applications to ask:

AI’s greatest value is not simply generating more applications at lower costs. It is making meaningful protection more accessible without sacrificing legal judgment.

Founders should ask how a firm coordinates its AI tools and legal expertise, not simply whether it uses AI. The answer should explain how that process improves both efficiency and substance.

Ultimately, the most expensive patent mistake may be discovering too late that a DIY application never adequately protected the innovation on which the business was built.

Michael Drapkin is a founding partner of Landfall IP, an AI-native patent law firm based in Boulder, Colorado. He previously founded one of the largest electronics and computer patent prosecution practices in the U.S. at an Am Law 100 firm and was named U.S. Patent Practitioner of the Year by Managing IP in 2022.

Nathan Mutter is managing partner and founder of Landfall IP, an AI-Native law firm. He has served as top outside counsel at Oura, Coinbase, Rubrik, Salesforce 1 and many others. Mutter pioneered the creation of automation systems and tools that enhance efficiency across all aspects of legal services. His clients include both startups and large public companies.

Bitcoin – How BTC could turn its recent price crash into an $87K rally

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Is Bitcoin’s [BTC] growing negative correlation to other risk assets becoming a key headwind?

It has been less than 48 hours since the most recent crypto wipeout resulted in over $1 billion in liquidations, pushing Bitcoin close to $70k, and sending market sentiment into extreme fear. While Bitcoin continues to trade below $75k, the S&P500 has risen by over 2%, pointing to stronger resilience despite the ongoing FUD.

This growing disparity raises questions about Bitcoin’s performance as a macro hedge this cycle. Notably, Peter Schiff shares his view. As his post below highlights, the long-time Bitcoin critic has warned of a deeper BTC correction, citing its underperformance compared to other risk assets. Even precious metals such as gold and silver have gained over 2%, further widening the performance gap.

Against this backdrop, positioning for further downside is starting to make more sense.

According to CoinGlass data, Bitcoin’s long/short ratio is showing bearish signs as it fell back from a 3-month high of 1.4 on the 7th of November, indicating traders are returning to shorting their positions, signaling weaker confidence in BTC’s near-term recovery. The key takeaway? There is no downside liquidity left to be hunted. The recent $1 billion liquidation event has wiped out almost all long-side liquidity.

This can be considered a great chance for bulls to recover. If Bitcoin remains above $75k and accumulation continues, then both early and late short positions could come under pressure. In fact, one analyst highlighted over $11 billion in short liquidity clustered around $87k.

Interestingly, bulls already seem to be positioning for this potential move.

Despite the scale of the recent wipeout, market sentiment is already showing signs of recovery.

The Crypto Fear & Greed Index shows that sentiment has improved by four points to 32. This indicates that selling pressure may be losing steam and that confidence is returning. Notably, institutional activity is also adding to this shift. As on-chain data reported, MSTR recently bought an additional 1,036 BTC.

To add to this, MARA Holdings has announced a Q3 net profit of $634.4 million, up from $123.1 million in the previous year. This is backed up by Santiment data showing a distinct increase in accumulation between retail and mid-sized holders. In the past two weeks the market has seen an increase of 1-10 BTC wallets by 10,076 BTC and 10-100 BTC wallets by 4,131 BTC.

In short, just because BTC has failed to outperform other assets does not mean that the rally is dying.

Despite macro uncertainty and bearish positioning being a key factor, there are clear signs of increasing institutional exposure and accumulation across wallet classes. If Bitcoin can hold above $75k, the current divergence could turn into a potential bear trap, with the $87k short-liquidity cluster becoming the next major upside target.

Bitcoin is lagging traditional markets, but institutional buying and whale accumulation remain strong.

If Bitcoin holds $75k, a short squeeze could push prices toward $87k.

China’s AI Diplomacy Is Working

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL REMLER is a Senior Fellow with the Technology and National Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. He served as AI Policy Coordinator in the Office of the Special Envoy for Critical and Emerging Technology at the State Department from 2023 to 2025.

Can the United States and China simultaneously cooperate and compete on AI? That is the question driving debates in Washington about the wisdom of pursuing talks on the subject with Beijing. The Biden administration paired sweeping technology restrictions with a narrow dialogue on risk, and the Trump administration has kept a channel open even as it has downplayed safety. Some experts go further. They hope that cooperation on safety can be siloed off from competition entirely, a belief as appealing as it is fanciful. Any dialogue on AI risk takes place within the broader strategic competition between the United States and China.

Beijing has long appreciated this dynamic. Much of Washington has not. Whereas the United States has shifted its approach to AI competition and safety across and within presidential administrations, Beijing has kept its AI diplomacy focused on gaining competitive advantage over the United States. China has taken part in international AI safety discussions, but it has used them, along with talks with the United States, to paint U.S. export controls as the main obstacle to cooperation. It has cast itself as the champion of the developing world, promising a Chinese-led AI ecosystem without technological or financial barriers as an alternative to a restrictive U.S.-led AI order. The pitch is working. A growing number of states are embracing China’s message, and Chinese open-weight models are gaining users across the developing world.

If the United States wants to maintain its lead and make meaningful progress on safety, it needs to treat the dialogue as part of the competition with China. To do so, it must ramp up pressure on China outside the talks and offer countries still choosing sides a more compelling vision for AI adoption and governance. And when U.S. and Chinese officials next meet, in November, Washington should test Beijing’s seriousness about safety. In short, the United States should approach AI diplomacy with the same strategic clarity China has.

The Biden administration’s first round of advanced semiconductor export controls in October 2022 and the release of ChatGPT a month later signaled that the United States had taken an early lead in the AI competition and would act to weaken China’s AI industry. Washington and its allies also seized the diplomatic initiative, taking the first steps toward global AI governance. In May 2023, the G-7 began intense discussions on AI governance that would eventually produce a code of conduct for developers. Weeks later, the United Kingdom announced it would host the first global AI safety summit at Bletchley Park.

China responded by rolling out a diplomatic script it has been honing ever since. Its core tenets first appeared in the Global AI Governance Initiative, published in October 2023. It called for countries to “put equal emphasis on development and security” (though it left security to be defined by individual states) and contrasted China’s focus on cooperation and development with U.S. export controls, attacking Washington for “creating barriers and disrupting the global AI supply chain through technological monopolies and unilateral coercive measures.” Despite Beijing’s growing body of AI regulations at home, the initiative offered little more than slogans on the technology’s risks.

That basic formula—cooperation and development, set against U.S. restrictions, with little to say about risk—has guided China’s AI diplomacy for the last three years. In March 2024, the UN General Assembly adopted a U.S.-led resolution on safe, secure, and trustworthy AI intended to appeal to both developed and developing countries. China supported the resolution, then introduced another resolution centered on help for poorer countries and “emphasizing that member states should enjoy equal opportunities” to develop and use AI. At a UN Security Council meeting on AI chaired by the United States in December that year, China’s UN ambassador, Fu Cong, once again counterposed Beijing’s stated interest in cooperation against “discriminatory barriers based on ideological differences.” In thinly veiled terms, Fu was charging that U.S. restrictions on China harmed the developing world.

To win the support of these countries as they built out their AI infrastructure, China needed a bloc of its own. In July 2025, Premier Li Qiang proposed the World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization (WAICO), which promised developing countries technology, training, and a greater say in writing global AI rules. In a speech marking its launch a year later, Chinese leader Xi Jinping called for “openness,” shorthand for access to U.S. chips and the adoption of Chinese open-weight models. After urging countries to take the risks of AI seriously, Xi added that they should, “in the meantime,” jointly oppose “overstretching the national security concept in the field of AI and placing one country’s security over that of others.” Cooperation on risks, in Xi’s telling, was subordinate to overcoming U.S. restrictions.

WAICO has quickly gained standing among its target audience. An early endorsement by UN Secretary-General António Guterres lent it immediate credibility in the eyes of developing countries, and Chinese officials have since promoted WAICO in frequent speeches and in bilateral meetings with countries as varied as Brazil, Pakistan, and Serbia. Beijing has signaled that membership will come with perks. Its draft export controls on leading models, released in October, promise to help WAICO’s 37 members build their AI capabilities, suggesting members could have easier access to models restricted for the rest of the world.

Xi personally led this diplomatic offensive in the lead-up to the Washington summit with U.S. President Donald Trump in September. During a speech in Kyrgyzstan, he pledged to expand a Chinese AI center for the Shanghai Cooperation Organization that helps members build local language models and train engineers. In Cairo, he and Egyptian President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi signed agreements on AI, data centers, and semiconductors, which have become a staple of Xi’s trips abroad. And in New Delhi, Xi proposed an open-source AI zone for BRICS members and told other leaders that a “small yard, high fences” approach would only “box oneself in and divide the world.” AI risks were nowhere on the agenda. The message instead was that U.S. restrictions would isolate Washington, not Beijing.

Much of the allure of China’s pitch rests on how the country’s developers share their technology. Chinese developers release their best models with open weights, which any government or company can download, run on its own hardware, and adapt to its own language. For governments wary of depending on American companies whose exports Washington can restrict, open-weight models promise a greater degree of control. Chinese developers rarely conduct or publish the results of any safety testing for these models, leaving others to discover after release whether, for example, they can be used to launch cyberattacks or build weapons. But their accessibility allows Beijing to argue that any effort to contain the risks of Chinese models is an attempt to deny AI to the developing world.

China’s approach to bilateral AI talks with the United States has deflected discussions of risk just as assiduously as its global AI diplomacy has. In May 2024, the United States and China held talks in Geneva, which I joined as part of the U.S. delegation. The U.S. objective of the talks was to test whether an in-depth technical exchange with China on AI risk was possible. The answer was no. Chinese diplomats steered the discussion away from risks for hours and declined to even explain China’s own AI regulations beyond a superficial overview, despite having technical experts from the relevant agencies in the room.

The Chinese position came through most clearly in the opening and closing remarks of the delegation’s most senior officials. They began with an appeal for cooperation on capacity-building projects in the developing world. They then cast U.S. technology restrictions as an attempt to contain China’s development and the U.S. emphasis on safety as a barrier preventing developing countries from accessing and using AI. The implication was clear—export controls were the primary impediment to cooperation on AI safety. The one concrete outcome, a November 2024 statement in which former President Joe Biden and Xi affirmed “the need to maintain human control over the decision to use nuclear weapons,” asked little of Beijing.

This September, after the summit between Trump and Xi in Washington, China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs published an “eight-point consensus,” Beijing’s account of what the two sides had agreed to. It described the dialogue as an exchange on the “risks and benefits” of AI. But whereas the White House framed the dialogue as a security issue, listing it alongside nuclear arms control, China’s Ministry of Commerce stressed instead that talks had taken place, and would continue, under the two countries’ economic and trade mechanism. Folded into trade talks, a dialogue on AI risks and benefits leaves Beijing free to parry U.S. requests on risks, such as limits on openly releasing models with dangerous capabilities, by demanding that Washington relax export controls.

Xi did propose to “jointly prevent the misuse and abuse of AI,” conveniently eliding China’s own frequent use of AI against the United States in various ways, such as hacking campaigns and influence operations. He added that AI should remain under human control and be developed for good, a principle Beijing has repeated since 2023 but about which it has offered little further detail or follow-through. More pointed was his insistence that future dialogue should “draw on each other’s strengths, not guard against each other” and his request that Chinese companies be “treated fairly,” both implicit rebukes of U.S. restrictions.

This record suggests Beijing will come to future meetings, beginning with the next discussion in November, with proposals designed to put Washington in a bind. Although U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer has made clear that export controls are not on the table, Beijing will raise them anyway. If Washington engages, Chinese negotiators will claim a standing channel where U.S. restrictions on AI technology are up for negotiation. If it refuses, Beijing will continue to evade U.S. entreaties on safety while accusing Washington of stifling opportunity for developing countries. It hopes that every round of the dialogue will further cement an impression among allies such as Japan and the Netherlands that Washington will trade away restrictions over their heads, and that they should accommodate China’s AI ambitions, too.

But Washington is in a stronger position than its diplomacy has let on. The world’s leading models are still American, and, together with allies, the United States controls key chokepoints in the semiconductor supply chain. Export controls on those chokepoints are not a bargaining chip and should be strengthened. Chinese AI companies regularly lament their lack of high-end chips and have resorted to smuggling chips and illicitly distilling U.S. models. U.S. developers also conduct increasingly extensive and transparent safety testing, which Chinese developers avoid. Taken together, these strengths give Washington the means to press Beijing and an attractive offer for the rest of the world. It should use them, advertising its rigorously tested models to compete for global adoption while pressuring China on safety in the dialogue.

The first chance to apply that pressure is the next bilateral meeting. Washington should set two conditions for it and future meetings. Chinese developers must commit to publishing safety evaluations at least as extensive as those that leading U.S. developers publish today on equivalent benchmarks for the most serious risks, such as cyberattacks and biological weapons, and to disclosing previous and future incidents. The United States can make the same commitments, as its companies already publish such evaluations, increasingly disclose incidents, and open their models to independent and government evaluators before release. Otherwise, the incident channel to which the two sides agreed in September will run one way, with China free to point to U.S. incidents while reporting none of its own.

These conditions would change the terms of the dialogue. Since 2023, Washington has raised risks and Beijing has steered the conversation toward development. As long as Chinese companies face no scrutiny for the risks of their models, Beijing can tout them for their openness and innovation without having to answer questions from the rest of the world about their safety. Concordia AI, which tracks China’s AI ecosystem, found that only five of China’s ten leading developers published safety test results for any model over the past year, and none did so consistently. If China agrees, governments and companies choosing which models to use will be able to judge Chinese and American developers by the same criteria. If China refuses, the onus will be on Beijing to explain why fair treatment for Chinese companies should not extend to safety testing. Regardless, the U.S. government should ramp up its own tests of new Chinese models and publish the results. It should also avoid unforced errors, such as Trump’s waving away of AI safety concerns as a “hoax,” which hand Beijing an easy retort to U.S. appeals.

More broadly, effective AI diplomacy ultimately requires convincing other countries to join the American AI ecosystem. Countries that train their engineers, build their local language models, and run their data centers on Chinese technology will come to depend on it. To counter that dependence (and prevent it from turning into political alignment), Washington needs to present a positive vision of AI governance that pairs verifiable safety commitments—the kind Chinese developers avoid—with the access and investment developing countries want.

U.S. companies already offer the best technology, but Washington has yet to put real diplomatic weight behind them. It should draw from its evolving approach at home to rally partners around shared safety measures. Under the White House Accord on Super Intelligence, signed in September, six leading U.S. companies committed themselves to internal controls, external audits, and independent oversight. The G-7 Code of Conduct, which built on voluntary commitments secured under the Biden administration, could incorporate the accord’s measures and serve as a common standard for partners. Countries that agree to its measures could receive priority for technology and financing. Without such incentives, the AI competition may be determined by price and access alone, where China has the edge.

Much of Washington has treated talks with Beijing as a place to manage the shared risks of AI apart from the competition. China has always treated them as an arena of the larger rivalry. To maintain its lead and make real progress on safety, the United States should start doing the same.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

How Africa’s Weak States and Rare Earths Drive Transnational Extremism

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

The Luxury of Children

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JESÚS FERNÁNDEZ-VILLAVERDE is Howard Marks Presidential Professor of Economics at the University of Pennsylvania. This piece is adapted from “Terra Incognita: The Economics of a Shrinking World,” co-authored with Patrick Norrick and forthcoming in the 2027 Annual Review of Economics.

Why the Baby Bust Is Making the World More Unequal

The world’s fertility crisis is more severe than is commonly understood. Last year, humanity’s total fertility level dropped below what demographers call the replacement rate—that is, the average number of children that a woman must have to keep a population constant over the long run. This has never happened before. There have been periods when the human population declined because of wars or epidemics, such as the Black Death, in the fourteenth century, or the diseases brought by Europeans to the Americas in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. But now, simply as a function of their choices, humans will see the world population peak around 2055 and then begin to fall.

The story of the coming decades is not simply one of dwindling and aging. There is a great deal of variation in the baby bust. The most surprising trend may be one that has overturned basic assumptions about fertility rates. Where once it was thought that people—and, by extension, countries—with higher levels of income and education would have lower fertility rates than those who were poorer and less educated, the inverse is increasingly true. Many wealthier people and countries boast higher fertility rates than many poor people and countries.

The inversion is revealing of why governments have had a hard time coming to grips with the fertility crash. The best explanation for why people are no longer having babies is that child-rearing at the scale necessary to maintain populations is fundamentally at odds with the structures of modern life and modern societies. Once an economic necessity, children are now an economic luxury. That portends a world in which economic inequality is accentuated by demographic inequality, with widening fissures within countries and among them. The whole world is grappling with a decline in fertility, but in many different ways. And those differences will stoke further divisions, tensions, and even conflict.

Humanity falling below replacement has passed largely unnoticed. The central projections in the latest release of the United Nations World Population Prospects (in 2024), known as the report’s “medium variant,” still places the world’s total fertility rate slightly above replacement in 2026 and expects the global population to peak as late as 2084. But this medium variant does not fully incorporate the recent drops in births recorded by national statistical agencies, and its projections for future fertility rest on questionable assumptions, including forecasting a strong recovery of fertility rates in countries such as China. By comparison, the report’s “low variant” (the forecast that takes a more pessimistic view of future fertility) accords nearly exactly with my own assessment, which reflects more recent national statistics and takes a less optimistic view on the possible recovery of fertility. In that reading, the world has dropped below replacement fertility levels (approximately 2.2 children per woman) and will only keep dropping.

Fertility rates are plunging so fast that they are overturning many assumptions about demography. The steepest fertility declines have occurred in unexpected countries. Consider this test. Pick the state with the highest fertility rate among the following: Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Jamaica, Iran, Malaysia, Thailand, Tunisia, Turkey, Sri Lanka, and the United States.

The United States, the wealthiest of the 12, has the highest fertility rate. This holds true even when just assessing the fertility rate of mothers born in the United States (immigrant mothers have a slightly higher fertility rate than native mothers). Dozens of other middle-income countries now have fertility levels below that of the United States.

That trend runs against the old explanation for demographic change, which held that fertility invariably falls as a community or society gets richer. That is demonstrably no longer the case. Among the countries in the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD), richer members tend to have higher fertility rates than do poorer ones, even after controlling for migration flows. Take Mexico. According to the most recent survey, in 2024, a Mexican woman could expect to have 1.2 children over her lifetime, well below the 1.57 average of non-Hispanic white women in the United States. Mexico’s income per capita is only about 28 percent of the U.S. level. And just two decades ago, fertility rates in Mexico were much higher than those in the United States.

This dynamic obtains not just among countries but also within them. Richer and more educated households in the United States and other advanced economies now tend to have more children than poorer and less educated ones, reversing a pattern that had held for centuries.

There is no single, simple answer for why this has happened. Most theories do not hold up against international evidence. Factors that seem to contribute to lower fertility in the United States, for instance, such as higher housing prices, don’t seem to matter elsewhere, as many countries have seen even steeper fertility declines without skyrocketing housing prices.

Or take another favorite culprit, smartphones. Some researchers have suggested that the spread of the smartphone stoked atomization and a desire for individual fulfillment, discouraging many people from having children. But fertility rates have been plunging worldwide since the late 1960s, well before smartphones appeared, and there is little evidence of an acceleration in the drop after their introduction. Instead, the device is more likely an accelerator of underlying mechanisms than a cause in itself: by my calculations, smartphones compressed into ten years a change in fertility that would have occurred anyway over 25.

Another common explanation for drops in fertility is the spread of female education and employment. Freed from the domestic function of birthing and raising babies, the argument goes, women have chosen to prioritize other goals instead of child rearing. Although that shift may indeed have played a role in the twentieth century in lowering fertility rates, it is no longer so clearly causal today. Indeed, across OECD countries, the correlation between fertility, on the one hand, and female education and employment, on the other, is positive: the countries where women are more educated and more likely to work outside the home are also the countries with higher fertility rates. And in other regions for which demographers have good vital statistics, such as Latin America, the women not having children in 2026 are the least educated and the ones most likely to not be part of the workforce.

In the absence of neat, definitive explanations, a broader explanation for the global fertility crash comes into view. As societies embrace what is often termed “modernity,” they become incapable of maintaining replacement-level fertility rates. Modernity describes how countries have organized contemporary life around formal institutions (such as schools, universities, and health systems), specialized expertise (through degree accreditation, standardized exams, and professional licensing), and large-scale systems of coordination (such as multinational corporations, governments, and global supply chains). It is not synonymous with capitalism; communist countries, too, invested heavily in mass education and bureaucracies (and saw concomitant declines in fertility). The only way to run large, complex societies that harness modern technology at scale is to standardize knowledge and detach it from local custom through mass education, systems of expertise, and bureaucracies.

Modernity raises the cost of children and lowers the cost of childlessness. Parents must equip children with formal knowledge and credentials, an endeavor that takes decades of effort and spending. Tight career schedules also conflict with parents’ biological clocks. The most productive years for a research economist, for example, are usually between 30 and 45. Those are also the years when one needs to spend the most time with children. No matter how much accommodation a university offers, the day still has only 24 hours. Even when a researcher manages to produce both a large family and strong academic output, one can always wonder how much more that researcher would have produced with a smaller family.

And modernity has also made it easier not to have children. In traditional agrarian and artisan economies, children were useful factors of production, helping in the fields and workshops. Children also provided parents and other older relatives with eldercare. But the modern organization of economic life no longer requires children to perform those roles and the advent of welfare states has given the elderly other forms of support. Lineage once determined people’s present and future; it no longer does so in such a comprehensive way.

There are some exceptions to this seemingly universal trend. Tight-knit religious communities, such as the ultra-Orthodox in Israel and the Amish in the United States, have managed to maintain high fertility rates well above replacement level. But these groups have also stayed apart from much of modern life. For example, the Amish live in rural areas, where housing is much cheaper, and they end formal education at eighth grade, which eliminates one of the main costs of raising a child in a modern society, advanced education.

Apart from these idiosyncratic pockets, modernity has spread around the planet, with fertility falling everywhere. But rates are tumbling unevenly. In 1980, the richer an OECD country, the fewer children its women had. By 2000, the opposite was beginning to become true. The same reversal shows up inside countries. In Sweden, the demographer Martin Kolk has found that in every cohort born between 1940 and 1970, men with higher lifetime incomes have more children, and that among women born since the 1960s, the poorest have the fewest children. In the United States, the economists Moshe Hazan and Hosny Zoabi have found that in the first decade of the twenty-first century, women with a graduate degree had about 2.0 children, significantly more than the 1.8 for women who stopped at a bachelor’s degree.

Many adults still want to have children and flourishing families. When Gallup asked Americans about their ideal number of children in July 2025, the average answer was 2.7, a figure that has barely changed since the 1970s. But children are costly and bring no income. They have become something of a luxury good that richer people can afford more of than poorer people can. In the same way that a richer household owns, on average, a more expensive car than a poorer one, it also has more children—at least since the 1990s. Prosperous parents can hire help at home, order prepared meals, pay for premium grocery delivery or laundry services, and afford tuition and tutoring.

Richer families (and, among them, the most educated parents) also have more stable family lives. For instance, among Americans born between 1957 and 1964 (a cohort whose adult lives can be observed almost in full), about half of men with a high school diploma but no college education had seen their first marriage end in divorce by age 55, compared with 27 percent of men with a bachelor’s degree or more. Stable families lead to higher fertility (and much better outcomes for the children). In short, income and education soften some of the worst consequences of modernity. The intuition of this result applies, as well, to countries as a whole: richer countries can, on average, afford more “luxuries,” including children, than poorer countries.

If the modernity conjecture is right, the flip in the correlation between income and fertility that is evident within OECD countries will extend to all other countries over the next few decades. This is already happening in the Americas: among countries with at least 20 million inhabitants, the United States has the highest fertility rate in the hemisphere, well ahead of Argentina, Brazil, Canada, Colombia, and Mexico, and matched only by Peru, whose fertility rate is plummeting fast. (Venezuela’s official fertility rate is pure fiction.)

The same phenomenon will occur in sub-Saharan Africa over the next few decades. It is often projected that the continent’s high fertility rates will make up for shortfalls elsewhere—but that will likely not happen. Indeed, in those sub-Saharan African countries for which demographers have slightly better statistical data, fertility rates are dropping very quickly. For example, a recent household survey in the Republic of Congo has shown a decrease in fertility from 5.1 children per woman in 2011–12 to 3.5 in 2025. Similar falls are happening elsewhere and likely to accelerate as sub-Saharan Africa succumbs to the same factors that have sped fertility declines elsewhere.

The coming years will produce deep changes in populations within countries and across countries. The already sharp divide between regions and social groups with higher average incomes and education levels and those with lower average incomes and education levels will grow more pronounced, with the added dimension of contrasting fertility rates. The school district of a wealthy suburb outside a major East Coast city may not need to grapple with the widespread school closings that urban districts are already experiencing. And since political power in a democracy follows, at least to some extent, the number of voters, these different regions and social groups will see their relative influence in American politics change: New York, for instance, has lost 19 votes in the electoral college since the 1940s.

Something similar can happen among countries. In 1972, nearly eight times as many children were born in China as in the United States. In 2025, that figure has dropped to 2.2 times as many. China’s total fertility rate in 2025 was 0.93, while the U.S. rate was 1.58. If fertility rates remain at those levels, the United States will overtake China in annual births at some point in the 2080s and in total population by around 2120. That date might arrive earlier if the gap between the fertility rates of China and the United States continues to widen.

Demography is not necessarily destiny, but a world in which the United States has a larger population than China is very different from the world today. The shrinking and aging of populations will have first-order effects on a country’s economic performance. In research with Gustavo Ventura and Wen Yao, I have documented that nearly all of Japan’s poor economic performance relative to the United States since 1990 can be explained by its worse demographics, not by financial crises or poor economic policy. Among the great powers, the United States is the least exposed to demographic headwinds. Younger populations tend to be more innovative and less reliant on public support, a fact that bodes well for American prospects in the competition to come.

But for most other countries, demographic decline will produce significant problems. Aging Japan, for instance, is likely the best-case scenario for countries reaching a demographic sunset. It boasts a capable state and a homogeneous society with a high degree of consensus. More important, Japan became rich before it got old. Countries such as Mexico and Thailand, which lack many of Japan’s advantages, may struggle to weather the same demographic headwinds. While rich economies can rely on immigration to soften some of the effects of falling fertility, countries such as Tunisia or Bolivia, which lose many people to emigration, may find it harder to deal with their own baby busts. The new dynamics of fertility augur a world in which gaps between the haves and the have-nots grow even wider, as poorer countries and communities endure strains that wealthier countries and communities manage to escape.

Short of an unlikely turn to the social mores of deeply conservative and ideological communities, such as the Amish, only transformations in material circumstances will help raise fertility rates. Governments have had a hard time determining how to tackle fertility decline. Policy can do only so much in convincing people to make the monumental decision to have children. But that does not mean that governments should not try to reverse these trends. Their policies should respect the freedom of citizens to form the families they believe are best for them. Ultralow fertility seems to be caused not by a lack of desire for children but by the absence of the conditions that would make having children easier, or even possible.

In this vein, some policies are no-brainers. Governments should make education systems more accessible and less competitive than, for example, the ones found in East Asia and in the United States, where high levels of cost and stress discourage having children. They should build more affordable housing and provide more affordable childcare. Such material changes may have only a marginal impact on rates, but even, for instance, raising the U.S. total fertility rate to 1.7 from the current 1.58 would produce a significant impact. Fertility rates behave like the rates of return on a retirement fund: small yearly differences accumulate into large differences over time. Policies that give people greater material freedom to have children can thus be very consequential in the long term.

If governments fail to enact these policies, and if, indeed, these policies fail to encourage a broader rise in fertility, the world will simply become more unequal. Richer families and richer countries will gain greater weight in the population, while poorer families and poorer countries dwindle and weaken. These shifts will create political tensions, as shrinking populations will add to their relative poverty the desperate sense that they have no demographic future. Eastern Germany offers a preview. Since reunification, its young people (above all, its young women) have left for western Germany, its towns have emptied and aged, and its voters have turned to the parties at both extremes of the political spectrum. Many countries in the world are about to become eastern Germany, but few of them will have a western Germany to pay the bills. Demographers often suggest that the world is united by the crash in fertility rates. In truth, demographic decline will likely divide the world even further.

Saylor Hayes' Cause of Death Revealed After Arrests of 5-Year-Old's Parents

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A cause of death for 5-year-old Saylor Hayes has been determined by Virginia and South Carolina authorities.

On Monday, November 9, the Washington County, Virginia, Sheriff’s Office announced findings from Georgetown County, South Carolina, coroners, who reported that Saylor died from asphyxia due to drowning, with child neglect as a contributing factor.

A manner of death is currently undetermined as a criminal investigation continues into the events surrounding Saylor’s death.

“This classification reflects the inability to conclusively determine whether Saylor’s death was accidental or the result of a homicidal act,” a Georgetown County Coroner’s Office spokesperson told WCBC on Monday.

Saylor was found dead in a waterway near her family’s Pawleys Island, South Carolina, rental home on August 31, one day after hundreds of locals and law enforcement officials took part in a large-scale search of the area.

The child’s parents, John Herb Hayes III and Geordyn Nichole Hayes, were arrested in Tennessee on September 1 and subsequently charged with unlawfully placing a child at risk/wilfully abandoning a child. (The couple have yet to enter a plea.)

“The body of the missing girl, who had been the subject of an intensive search involving hundreds of people and multiple agencies, was found yesterday morning in the waters near Hagley Landing,” police announced that same day. “Once extradition proceedings are complete, the couple will be transported back to Georgetown County.”

An arrest affidavit laid out accusations that John, 70, and Geordyn, 31, put their daughter “at an unreasonable risk of harm affecting her life and safety by failing to provide supervision and safeguards appropriate for a severely nonverbal, autistic, 5-year-old child with a known affinity to water and a history of elopement.”

Investigators determined that Saylor was not wearing a Project Lifesaver transmitter at the time of her disappearance. She usually wore the device because she tended to wander off when she was unaccompanied by an adult. (The transmitter was allegedly removed before Saylor left her family’s Virginia home though it’s not clear why.)

John and Geordyn were accused by police of complicating search efforts by urging law enforcement to track her Project Lifesaver transmitter.

“[They] encouraged search efforts to be directed toward the use of tracking technology, despite knowing that the child was not wearing the monitoring device,” a warrant read. “[They] further directed search efforts away from the nearby body of water.”

At the time, Georgetown County Sheriff Carter Weaver suggested that the Hayeses may have violated a “fundamental responsibility to protect children who cannot protect themselves.”

“When the evidence establishes probable cause to believe that a child has been unlawfully placed in circumstances that endanger that child’s safety, law enforcement has a responsibility to act,” the sheriff said in a September 1 statement.

Weaver added that Saylor was a “vulnerable five-year-old child who depended upon the adults responsible for her care and protection.”

“She deserved to be safe. She deserved to be protected,” he continued. “These arrests are based upon the evidence developed during this investigation and the applicable law. They should not be interpreted as a determination of guilt. The defendants are presumed innocent unless and until proven guilty in a court of law.”

Following her daughter’s death, reports emerged that Geordyn was allegedly charged with child abuse over a completely separate matter six months before Saylor’s death. That case revolved around accusations of child abuse/neglect, plus reckless disregard for life, following an alleged incident with a juvenile male.

If you or someone you know is experiencing child abuse, call or text Child Help Hotline at 1-800-422-4453.

How A Salesman Accidentally Convinced Shaquille O'Neal To Buy 3 Ridiculously Expensive Cars

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Shaquille O'Neal doesn't do small or understated. The 7-foot-1 four-time NBA champion has an instantly recognizable brand. Oh, he's worth around $500 million, too. Naturally, as with many other well-to-do star athletes, he has a penchant for flashy cars. Problem is, he seldom fits in them without some contortions and modification. It's what drove him to have a Lamborghini lengthened into a Franken-Gallardo. That stretched supercar isn't the only time Shaq did some silly stuff with expensive cars.

In what was almost certainly a prideful purchase, O'Neal bought three outrageously expensive cars on the spot. Why? All it took was one condescending question from a less-than-respectful Rolls-Royce dealership salesman, and Shaq bought the lot. Just like that, the champ and business mogul had three extra opulent cars he says "I never drive."

Don't believe it? One of them, a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom, just sold on Bring-a-Trailer. If that wasn't enough, the once-undisputed heavyweight champion of the world, Mike Tyson, may have had a hand in nudging the Diesel to buy three ultraluxe cars instead of a solitary sedan. Of course, it's not difficult to get carried away when shopping for Rolls-Royces; just look at the one-off Phantom inspired by "Goldfinger."

Some 30 years ago, Shaquille O'Neal signed a groundbreaking $120 million deal to don Los Angeles Lakers colors and put away his Orlando Magic jersey. Several years later, he wandered into a Rolls-Royce showroom with his friend Alex and started browsing the hand-built grandeur.

Now, O'Neal had money to spare, but he wasn't quite up for indiscriminate spending. "I like to spend money, but I don't like to waste money. So I don't want to spend $400,000 for a car," O'Neal recalled in a Revolt interview. He went about asking a salesperson about the prices of the showroom's many Roll-Royces. That's when the salesperson said the wrong thing. Or the right thing, considering the commission on three hilariously expensive cars.

In O'Neal's book, "Shaq Uncut: My Story,"  he recalls the question as especially disrespectful, be it accidental or deliberate. The salesperson gave the Lakers sweatpants-clad O'Neal a glance and, per Basketball Network, asked, "Are you sure you can afford it, sonny boy?" That did it. Shaq lost it. "I said, 'What the f*** did you just say to me?'" So, he pointed around the showroom. "I want that silver hardtop one, and I want the green one, and give that red one over there, too."

One of those cars, the silver hardtop O'Neal selected, was a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom. That car sold in November 2026 on Bring-a-Trailer for $62,000. Not exactly the $320,000 or so O'Neal likely paid for it when it was new in 2004. Although he was shopping in a Rolls-Royce dealer, Shaq's book mentions a handful of Bentleys as part of the splurge. Whichever British luxury marque made O'Neal's list of picks, it was quite the spendy visit to a dealership.

Shaq's financial flexing and what is likely the most impressive commission that Rolls-Royce salesperson has ever made makes for a pretty crazy story. Believe it or not, it gets even more wild from there. According to O'Neal's book, "Iron" Mike himself, Mike Tyson, was in the mood for Bentley shopping just as the Laker found himself at the dealership.

Shaq recalled seeing Tyson picking out a couple of Bentleys and the need to out-do the champion pugilist. "Next thing I know Tyson picked out two. He's buying two of these cars, so you know what I've got to do. I've got to buy three!" Though it's not clear what ratio of Rolls-Royce to Bentleys made up his impulse shopping spree.

Be it Iron Mike or the mouthy salesperson who drove Shaq to pull the trigger, the outlandishly expensive price tag for the purchase hit him after. "A million dollars gone in 10 minutes," the massive athlete once lamented in an interview with Vanity Fair. Frankly, that's not the only time O'Neal has had the rotten luck with luxury cars. After spending the money to have it customized to fit his considerable frame, his Lucid Air Coupe ended up in a salvage yard less than two years later. Bummer.

Gordon Ramsay Shows Off Abs in Shirtless Photo for 60th Birthday

Gordon Ramsay is only getting better with thyme.

In the Nov. 9 Instagram picture, Ramsay proved that, unlike his vegetables, he is not chopped as he stood nearly-naked in his workout gear and flexed for the camera, showing off his toned physique.

Arbitrum added $64.2M in stablecoin trading in a week, but what does this mean for ARB?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Arbitrum’s [ARB] has had a busy week, at least as far as stablecoins are concerned. And, it’s probably going to get busier now. Especially now that there’s a new privacy tool on the network, giving people more reasons to show up.

Arc was in second place, with $11.8 million. Ink added $4.2 million, and Tempo $2.7 million. After that, it fell off a cliff. OP Mainnet, zkSync, and Gnosis each grew by less than $200K, and Boba added under $20K.

Every chain from second to eighth together will get you roughly $19.2 million. And yet, Arbitrum’s number is more than three times that.

Here, it’s worth pointing out that this chart is a measure of growth. It is a measure of who added the most stablecoin trading in a week. Not who’s the biggest overall.

NullMask, a privacy protocol, is now live on the network. Users can send, swap, deposit and withdraw crypto, and even check their balances, without putting the transaction details out in public.

Normally, anyone with a block explorer can see all of that.

There’s been more appeal for this on Layer 2s, especially from those who value their privacy.

Now, whether it actually brings more users and volume is still an open question. However, it’s still reason enough to stick around.

ARB has had a decent run on the price charts lately.

After dipping to about $0.160 late on 8th November, ARB went up to $0.188 by next afternoon. It’s since pulled back a bit and at press time, ARB was trading near $0.1795.

That pointed to 12% above the dip, comfortably clear of the range it had been in for most of the near-term.

The RSI was close to 77 at the top; before it fell to to 58.2 later under its Signal Line at 64.1. The CMF is the one to watch though. It was barely below zero, after shortly going green during the rally. The price went up, but real buying pressure didn’t really follow.

The move has a better shot at sticking if ARB climbs above $0.172.

The Trade Desk’s Revenue Slipped In Q3. If It Wants To Grow, It Must Evolve

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

The Trade Desk’s total revenue last quarter was $736 million, down a smidge from $739 million a year ago. Profit, however, fell far more sharply, from $116 million to $38 million.

The ground is rapidly shifting beneath The Trade Desk’s feet. Take, for example, who is now calling the shots on media buys.

“Decisions about what advertising to buy that were once made primarily by hands-on-keyboard traders are increasingly being made by senior agency leaders, procurement groups at large brands and senior executives,” TTD CEO Jeff Green told investors on the company’s Q3 earnings call Monday evening.

Meanwhile, the knowledge gap has narrowed. It was not that long ago when a CMO might not have known their own brand’s programmatic setup or even what a log file is.

Nowadays, though, even CFOs and CEOs often know, or are quickly learning, “the esoteric details of programmatic advertising,” Green said.

All of this matters for TTD because it’s now dealing with new types of people within the marketing and leadership orgs.

Where those buyers put their money is changing, too. Video, which includes CTV, online video and mobile video, is now a low 50% share of total spend. Mobile is in the high 20% range, while display is only a low double-digit percentage. Audio, which Green said was TTD’s fastest-growing channel by percentage, was close behind display at 7% of the business in Q3.

Notably, CTV and retail media, which normally dominate investor calls, barely came up. Instead, Green, other TTD execs and investors focused on smaller channels and business lines.

Political spend on the platform this year, for example, actually topped the 2024 presidential race. “You can argue this was our best political cycle yet,” Green said.

TTD’s newly appointed CFO Nate Olmstead told investors that The Trade Desk “saw particular demand strength in medical health and the political election business.” The company also touted healthcare measurement partnerships with Veeva Crossix and IQVIA Digital, as well as an integration with OptimizeRx for medical ad inventory and targeting.

Then there’s the global business. TTD has always indexed heavily toward North America and US Fortune 1,000 marketers. But as growth in that region has plateaued or even tipped downward, the company’s APAC business was up 30% in the first nine months of the year versus last year, led by 45% growth in China. EMEA is up more than 20%.

And then, of course, there’s the agentic revolution.

“AI has produced a tremendous opportunity for us,” Green said. Thanks to AI, he said, TTD is refining its product faster than ever. “AI is helping us operate, AI is helping us build product and AI is in the product to help our clients manage their advertising.”

To be fair, Green also had more tangible metrics. Clients are setting up workflows faster, and time spent creating a campaign is down 9% on average, with a 15% reduction for ad group creation and another 9% for uploading creatives. The agentic platform is in beta, Green said, but “early adopters highlight meaningful time savings.”

And buyers would be wise to get familiar with it. The new agentic UI “is a good example of where the platform is heading,” Green said, and programmatic is one of the best business use cases for agentic chatbots and AI optimization.

Green described the platform as an advertiser API that gives “people quadrillions of permutations for every single campaign,” adding that it can be “overwhelming for you to figure out what dial to turn and what to do.” In simple terms, he said, the value prop of an agentic platform is a data feed, a few easy controls and AI that manages the thousands of dials that can be tuned a million different ways.

With the agentic future (the agentic present?) in mind, even TTD’s hitherto reserved approach to M&A could change. (The Trade Desk has two known acquisitions, Adbrain in 2017 and Sincera last year, both more like small acqui-hires. But a third acquisition came between those two deals, and its details remain undisclosed.)

But the company is “always looking,” Green said, even if it hasn’t been an active acquirer.

“I will just transparently say we’re looking more now than we historically have,” Green added. “Partly because I just think there’s a bunch of opportunity in the market that there hasn’t been in the past.”

Investors on the call seemed pleased that The Trade Desk had surpassed its low forecast, though they remain wary of the programmatic open web.

“Our growth is still below the level we expect from ourselves and what we think we are capable of,” Green told them, “and, honestly, what the ecosystem needs from us.”

No major retail media player has taken as circumspect an approach to ad revenue as Shopify. But the company has recently taken its next important step with a product called Campaign Autopilot, VP of product for merchant marketing Samir Pradhan tells AdExchanger.

OpenAI’s head of monetization, Benji Shomair makes the case for ads as a way to bring ChatGPT to more people, with user trust taking priority over advertiser demands.

Viant is doubling down on CTV with its $20 million Waymark acquisition, betting that AI-generated ads can bring more advertisers to streaming TV.

TIME is using AI agents to draft proposals and troubleshoot campaigns – but not to replace the human relationships that drive ad sales.

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Amy Poehler And Steve Carell Bonded Over How Poorly The Office And Parks And Rec Went Their First Seasons

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

Get the latest movie, TV and streaming news delivered to your inbox.

Thank Goodness A Nobody Wants This Star And I Have The Same Weird Emotions About Morgan And Sasha

I Loved That St. Denis Medical Straight Up Referenced The Pitt, And I Had To Ask The Creator Why

I Started Watching Big Brother Because Of Survivor, But Producers Were Shook By All That Fan Hate

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If Parks and Recreation and The Office have one thing in common, it's that both series took a little while to find their footing. Of course, these two comedies have more than one thing in common -- both aired on NBC around the same time, both were created (or co-created in Parks & Rec's case, alongside Michael Schur) by Greg Daniels, and both debuted with big expectations from fans of an existing series. It's no surprise that Amy Poehler would find some very common ground with Steve Carell when talking about their experiences on their respective shows.

Not all of the best sitcoms of all time got off to an amazing start. The Office and Parks and Recreation are two perfect examples of shows that needed more than a few episodes to find their groove. In March, back when Steve Carell's new HBO series Rooster was just getting started, the Office star sat down with Amy Poehler on her Good Hang podcast. Among the topics that came up was how poorly their earlier sitcoms were initially received by viewers.

As Poehler tells it, there are two people that she's been told she's a "poor man's version of," and Carell is one of them. She went on to laugh about how terribly the debut of Parks and Recreation went, saying:

Parks and Rec had the worst launch ever. Everyone was like, 'This is not The Office, we don't like this.' I just remembered we're like -- 'You're not Steve and you're not -- we don't like it!' And I think I just dissociated and was like, 'well.'"

That viewers might have been looking for more of what they'd come to know and love from The Office -- and Carell's character, Michael Scott -- is not that surprising, given its creators and the fact that it was another single-camera mockumentary-style workplace comedy. In the case of Parks and Rec, the focus was on the office workers at the Parks and Recreation department of a small town in Indiana. That included Poehler's optimistic and determined Leslie Knope.

When Greg Daniels and Michael Schur's new series debuted in spring of 2009, it was around the same time that The Office was wrapping up its fifth season. Months prior to Parks and Rec's debut, The Office had its post-Super Bowl double episode, "Stress Relief," which was watched by millions (and is remembered as one of the best episodes of the series). The Office was firmly established with its fanbase, while Parks and Rec was just getting warmed up.

It's almost easy to forget that The Office had faced a similar challenge years earlier, as Carell noted in his conversation with Poehler:

Our pilot was the lowest casting pilot in the history, I think, of NBC. People really hated it ... They actively hated this show, and I don't quite know how it got legs after that.

Lest we not forget, The Office didn't debut to universal praise and sky-high ratings when it arrived in 2005, and it was nearly cancelled after its first season. The show arrived on the heels of the success of the 2001 Ricky Gervais sitcom that started it all, and to say that the U.S. version of the beloved UK comedy had big shoes to fill would be an understatement.

Personally, I didn't hate the first season of the U.S. version of The Office when it aired, but I also wouldn't argue with many of the criticisms of the show's first six episodes. That's a whole other conversation though. Getting back to the topic at hand, in his conversation with Amy Poehler, Carell called himself out as "a poor man's Ricky Gervais."

I don't think anyone would say that about Carell today, but he certainly had a high bar to meet when leading the U.S. version of The Office. And from what he said, he intentionally didn't watch more than a minute of Gervais in Office:

I watched like a minute of him, and he was so good, and so specific, and so funny, I thought, if I watch a second more, I'm just going to go on an audition with that. I won't be able to even imagine it a different way.

There really is only one David Brent, so it's understandable that Carell wouldn't want to try to recreate that character in Michael Scott. Ultimately, Carell's character became iconic in his own right, thanks in large part to the actor's comedic brilliance. Several seasons after he made his debut as Michael Scott, Carell had a say in how his character would exit the series.

All of this to say, I can see why Carell and Poehler would connect on this particular topic, as both of them saw success in their respective roles and shows, but it wasn't instant and both had to find their way out from under the shadow of other performances. Each show had to find their own way to success, and it's just really fortunate that both were given time and seasons to do so. I can't imagine a TV world without Michael Scott or Leslie Knope!

You can watch Parks and Recreation and The Office streaming with a Peacock subscription.

Robert Pattinson's James Bond Comments Resurface 14 Years Later Amid Casting Rumors

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Fresh off his electric performance in the dark, unsettling media satire "Primetime," Robert Pattinson is said to be in contention for the role of James Bond. According to The Hollywood Reporter, Pattinson screen tested for the part in Denis Villeneuve's upcoming Bond reboot movie for Amazon MGM Studios, which puts him in the running alongside several other well-known contenders. For the actor, it has been a long time coming. It seems that, 14 years ago, Pattinson envisioned stepping into the 007 role at a later point in his career.

In a 2012 interview with The Sun (via NDTV), the British star said he'd "definitely like to go for Bond" but wanted to wait another 20 years. "There'd be nothing worse than, like, 'Let's get a fresh-faced Bond!'" he added. "That would be the worst idea in the world. It would be ridiculous to reinvent it as some young posh kid." At the time, he was just starting to make his transition from teen heartthrob to chameleonic leading man, and was promoting what has since become known as one of Pattinson's best movies, David Cronenberg's "Cosmopolis." Since then, much has changed, and while it hasn't quite been two decades, it seems the actor wasn't kidding when he said he'd one day like to play 007.

Back in October 2026, it was reported that Josh O'Connor was one of the Bond finalists, alongside "Fantastic Beasts" actor Callum Turner, and "Slow Horses" star Jack Lowden. Since then, reports have claimed that Nicholas Hoult and Pattinson are also in the final group, which means five actors are now vying for the role of England's greatest spy. If Pattinson does land the role, it will be yet another major success for the actor and, given his prior comments, a particularly satisfying one.

In his interview with The Sun, Robert Pattinson seemed entirely opposed to the idea of a young James Bond. "After Daniel Craig you have to have some baggage," he said of the erstwhile Bond actor, who reinvented the spy for a new generation with the unbeatable "Casino Royale" in 2006. "I'd have to be tortured in the first few scenes. I'd have to do the first film with one arm or something." It's unclear just how serious Pattinson was at the time, but he seemed to have an awareness of just how tortured a version of Bond Craig portrayed during his tenure in the tux.

With that in mind, it will be interesting to see, if Pattinson does land the part, whether he doubles down on the haunted loner version of 007 he mentioned in his 2012 comments. The best Bonds have all had an edge to them, but it might be that Denis Villeneuve and Amazon MGM want to go in a different direction after the diminishing returns of the Craig era Bond films.

As it stands, nobody knows for sure who will play the lead role in Villeneuve's upcoming film. Based on the finalists, one thing we do know is that Amazon won't be going the "young posh kid" route, to use Pattinson's phrasing. All the contenders are 36 years old, except for Pattinson who at 40 is the oldest of the bunch. With final screen tests said to have already taken place, it won't be long before we get an official announcement, and if Pattinson does land the role we at least know he's been mentally preparing for 14 years.
